 Meeting of the Board  
30 June – 3 July 2025  
Port Moresby, Papua New Guinea  
Provisional agenda item 1 1 GCF/B. 42/02 /Add. 16 
9 June 2025   
Consideration of funding proposals – 
Addendum XVI 
Funding proposal package f or FP 274 
 
Summary  
This addendum contains the following six parts:  
a) A funding proposal titled “Building the Climate Resilience of Children and 
Communities through the Education Sector (BRACE) ”; 
b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal 
point(s);  
c) Secretariat ’s assessment;  
d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel ’s assessment;  
e) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's 
assessment; and  
f) Gender documentation.

Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template  
• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross -
reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.  
• Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details 
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annex es must be 
reflected in all relevant documents.  
• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not 
exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the  usual 
service standard time.  
• The rec ommended font is Arial, size 11. out  
• Under  the GCF Information Disclosure Policy , project and programme funding proposals 
will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, 
subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. 
Accredited Entities are asked to fill out  information on disclosure in section G.4 .    
 
Please submit the completed proposal to: 
fundingproposal@gcfund.org 
Please use the following name convention for the file name: 
“FP-[Accredited Entity  Short Name]- [Country/Region] -[YYYY/MM/DD ]”

A 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 3 
3 
 1. Globally, one billion children, nearly half the world’s children, live in countries at extreme vulnerability to the impacts 
of climate change4. All three countries targeted by BRACE through Component 1 -  Cambodia, South Sudan, and 
Tonga - are exposed to increasing temperatures, changing rainfall patterns, and extreme events (e.g., droughts, 
floods, and high/extreme temperature events) which result  in a range of education- related impacts. The main 
impacts the project will address include climate- related increases in: children dropping out of school, children and 
teachers absent from school, learning loss and impacts on learning outcomes, and gender -related impacts (e.g., 
increases in gender -based violence), and physical impacts that climate hazards have on school facilities (e.g. 
water, sanitation and hygiene ( WASH ) impacts, etc).   
 
2. Component 1  will directly address and respond to the impacts of climate change on the education sector and 
address future climate risks in Cambodia, South Sudan, and Tonga . The project combines national -, sub-national -, 
and school -level interventions to create sufficient conditions for scalability, and sufficient integration to catalyse the 
transition to a climate- resilient education system.  In Output 1 .1 – Enabling systems and policies are in place and 
supported, climate and education policies, plans and guidelines will be prepared with Ministries of Education and Environment (Activity 1.1.1), and coordination on climate change within Ministries of Education (MoEs ), and other 
relevant line ministries will be strengthened  (Activity 1.1.2) . Disaster Risk Reduction (DRR) plans will be facilitated, 
tested and updated (Activity 1.1.3), and children will be supported to participate in climate change and education policy initiatives (Activity 1.1.4).  In Output 1.2, school facilities are safer and greener, national standards for climate-
resilient school facilities will be prepared ( Activity 1.2.1), and school facilities, including WASH, retrofitted to 
strengthen climate resilience (Activity 1.2.2). To ensure sustainability, s chool communities and education officials 
will be supported to operate and maintain the retrofitted school facilities  (Activity 1.2.3). In Output 1.3, school safety 
and educational continuity management systems are operating effectively, master trainers from the Ministry of Education will be supported (Activity 1.3.1) to train school management , teachers and children in how to prepare 
climate -related school s afety plans  (Activity 1.3.2) , with schools supported to implement their plans and update 
their plans annually throughout the duration of the project (Activity 1.3.3).  Climate resilience tools, equipment and 
kits will also be distributed to schools (Activity 1.3.4), and Early Warning Systems (EWS) and Climate Information Systems (CIS) access and use in schools will be strengthened (Activity 1.3.5).  In Output 1.4, climate change 
resilience teaching and learning embedded in national systems will be supported through the development of teaching and learning materials on climate change (Activity 1.4.1), building the capacity of teachers and education manager s to implement climate change lessons inside the classroom (Activity 1.4.2), support t o child clubs to lead 
climate change initiatives in schools and communities (Activity 1.4.3), and uploading climate change teaching and learning materials to e- learning and other platforms (Activity 1.4.4). Learning documents and events with Ministries 
of Education to share experience of integrating climate change into the curriculum will also be held (Activity 1.4.5).  
The project will directly benefit 607 ,098 people in Component 1, including 392,185 people (1 88,373 female) in 
Cambodia, 201, 242 (104, 929 female) in South Sudan, and 13, 671 (6,6 31 female in Tonga ) and a total of 850 
schools  across all three countries (see Annex 2: Section 6.2: Target Areas and Beneficiaries  and Annex es17a and 
17b).   
 
3. In Component 2 , the project will support  Ministries of Education in the three countries  – Cambodia, South Sudan 
and Tonga - supporting them to further access climate finance and to lead climate- resilient sustainable 
development in the education sector. This will include the development of education sector risk analyses on the 
impact of climate  change on the education sector and children’s learning (Activity 2.1.1), MoEs to engage in 
National Adaptation Plan (NAP) processes, including those that may feed into global decisions such as those of the UNFCCC. MoEs wi ll also be supported to develop and implement climate finance mobilization road maps, with 
Direct Access Entities (DAEs) supported to  prepare concept notes for the GCF focused on the education sector. 
Direct beneficiaries will be 210 Ministry of Education and other education and climate change officials from the 
three countries, with the impact potential to reach children and education personnel in each country.   
 
4. Component 3 , will support the education community to better coordinate, collaborate, and share lessons learned 
and experiences with a focus on climate finance. T he project will ensure that education and climate stakeholders 
are connecting, coordinating and cross -learning on climate finance  for the benefit of the BRACE countries and 
future scaling up through the GPE parallel project financ e and broader education systems strengthening work . This 
will be done by establishing a BRACE Climate and Education Financing Coordination Platform, including a steering 
committee and core team,  to drive forward the interventions  (Activity 3.1.1) . In addition, Ministries of Education and 
Environment from Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga will participate in face -to-face meetings to support cross -
learning and build political momentum for climate and education (Activity 3.1.2).  Global climate finance 
opportunities in the education sector will be mapped and tracked (Activity 3.1.3), and adjustments to internati onal 
climate finance reporting to reflect the dual -benefit of investments in climate and education will be undertaken 
(Activity 3.1.4). The parallel project financ e-supported knowledge management platform in a climate change space 
will also be leveraged to support the three countries access and share learning  (Activity 3. 2.1) and guidance 
developed  on climate change and education for the GCF, as well as the global education community on climate

4 
 finance (Activity 3.2.2) will be developed. Webinars, thematic sessions and learning events will also be held to 
support the BRACE countries (Activity 3.2.3), and strategic communications materials on climate and education 
prepared and disseminated (Activity 3. 2.4). Component 3 of BRACE will directly reach 1,000 beneficiaries  including 
education and climate change actors in the three target countries . The project’s goal statement is   IF the education 
stakeholders in climate- vulnerable countries are better informed of climate risks and adaptation options for the 
education sector, as well as have access to global knowledge, policy exchange and financing, and IF the target 
schools are teaching children about climate change and become safer and greener, THEN  the school systems of 
the target countries and beyond, including children and communities, become more resilient, BECAUSE , policies 
of the education sector becomes more responsive to clim ate change, there will be increased climate finance for 
the sector, Ministry of Education staff, students and communities will become more knowledgeable and engaged 
in climate adaptation actions and advocacy.

 PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION  
B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)  
5. South Sudan  is expected to face significant climate impacts under the SSP2 -4.5 scenario from 2020 to 2050, with critical 
implications for school -age children. These impacts include increased rainfall variability, heightened risks of flooding, 
intensified heat extremes, and changes in drought severity, all of which will profoundly affect water availability, agriculture, 
and health.  
 
6. Rainfall is projected to increase slightly, with annual totals averaging around 1,236 mm and modest upward trends. 
Extreme rainfall events, such as one- day and five- day maximum rainfall, are expected to intensify significantly. Regions 
like Rumbek North and Akobo in the central areas face heightened flood risks, with severity anomalies reaching up to 100 mm during peak events. Southeastern areas, such as Kapoeta East, are likely to experience moderate rainfall increases. The variability in rainfall patterns  and extremes will exacerbate flood risks, disrupting infrastructure and access to schools, 
especially during the wet season.  
 
7. South Sudan will experience a significant warming trend, with mean temperatures rising by 0.33°C per decade. By mid-
century, maximum temperatures will exceed 42°C in some areas, with extreme heat days above 35°C projected to 
increase by over 9 days per decade. These changes will disproportionately affect northern regions like Rubkona and Twic, where temperature anomalies are expected to exceed +1.25°C. For school -age children, extreme heat will heighten risks 
of heat -related illnesses, dehydration, and redu
ced ability to concentrate, particularly in poorly ventilated or outdoor 
learning environments.  
 
8. Despite projected increases in rainfall, drought intensity is expected to decrease in many areas, reflected by improvements in the SPEI drought index. However, rainfall variability and changes in wet and dry spell duration present challenges. 
Central and northern regions will see slight reductions in consecutive dry days, but the southeastern regions will face more significant decreases in dry spell durations. Th
ese changes may improve water availability but could disrupt 
traditional planting cycles, impacting food security and nutrition —a critical concern for children’s development and 
learning.  
 
Figure 1-1: Projected flooding and extreme rainfall characteristics. Flood areas (left), extreme rainfall anomaly characteristics (righ t) (Map source: United Nations 
OCHA HDX Platform, 18 Dec 2024)

3.2. 
9. Cambodia's  climate projections under the SSP2- 4.5 scenario from 2020 to 2050 indicate significant changes in rainfall 
patterns, temperature increases, and heightened risks of extreme weather events. These changes will profoundly affect 
water management, agriculture,  and public health, with notable impacts on school -age children.  
 
10. Annual rainfall is projected to increase slightly, averaging 1,791.78 mm with a 1% per decade upward trend, but regional 
and seasonal differences will result in uneven impacts. Central regions are expected to receive more rainfall, while parts 
of the northeast, southeast, and northwest may face declines. Seasonal rainfall patterns will shift, with wetter monsoons from July to October and reduced rainfall in critical planting months like April and May. Extreme rainfall events, such as 
five-day and one- month 
maximums, are projected to intensify, increasing flood risks, particularly in southern and 
northeastern areas.  
 
11. Cambodia will experience a significant warming trend, with minimum, mean, and maximum temperatures increasing by 
0.27–0.28°C per decade. By mid- century, average maximum temperatures will reach 32.62°C, with inland areas seeing 
the highest anomalies. The number of hot days exceeding 35°C will rise dramatically, from 67 days annua lly to over 90 
days, particularly in central and northwestern regions. Days above 40°C, though rare, will also increase.  
 
12. This sustained heat poses significant risks to school -age children, including heat -related illnesses, dehydration, and 
reduced learning performance due to discomfort in poorly ventilated classrooms. Prolonged exposure to extreme 
temperatures can also disrupt sleep, affecting children’s cognitive function and academic achievement.  
 
13. While the drought index (SPEI) shows an overall improvement, indicating fewer and less severe droughts, regional 
disparities persist. The northwest may face increased drought severity, while other areas experience moderate 
improvements. Rising aridity and evaporation rates in the northwest could exacerbate water scarcity, impacting agriculture 
and household water supply.    20.0  21.0  22.0  23.0  24.0  25.0  26.0  27.0

 For more data, maps and graphs on projected climate change impacts in Cambodia see Annex 2 feasibility study section 2.2. 
 
14. Tonga's  climate projections under the SSP2- 4.5 scenario reveal nuanced changes in rainfall, extreme weather events, 
and temperature patterns that will significantly affect its people and ecosystems. These changes pose distinct challenges 
for school -age children, who are particularly vulnerable to disruptions in education, health, and well -being. Tonga is 
expected to experience a slight decrease in annual rainfall, averaging 1,732.93 mm, with more pronounced variability and intensified extreme rainfall events. The northern islands are likely to face higher extremes, with one- day and five- day 
maximum rainfall totals increasing by nearly 1 mm and 1.36 mm per decade, respectively. Rainfall seasonality will 
intensify, causing greater year -to-year variability and potenti ally disrupting water availability and agricultural cycles. For 
children, this could mean disruptions to schooling due to infrastructure damage or unsafe travel conditions during extreme 
rainfall events, especially in remote areas.  
 
15. While drought conditions are projected to improve slightly, increasing aridity and evaporation in some regions could strain 
water resources, essential for both schools and households. Such conditions may lead to water shortages, which can 
directly impact hygiene and nutrition—key determinants of children’s health and cognitive development.  
 
16. Tonga will face significant warming trends, with minimum, mean, and maximum temperatures rising by approximately 
0.17°C per decade. By 2050, the number of days with temperatures above 25°C is expected to rise substantially, with 
such conditions becoming the norm for over 270 days annually. Northern regions will experience longer warm spells, with durations extending by 50 days per year, while maximum daytime temperatures are projected to exceed 29°C in western 
areas.  
 
17. The frequency of category 3 cyclones is projected to decrease slightly, but tropical cyclones remain a significant threat to 
Tonga, given their potential for widespread destruction. Schools are particularly vulnerable to damage from cyclones, 
which can dis place children, interrupt education, and exacerbate mental health challenges due to the trauma of extreme 
weather events.

OCHA HDX Platform, 18 Dec 2024)

Impacts on School -Age Children, School Infrastructure, and Learning 
18. The projected climate changes for South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga under the SSP2- 4.5 scenario highlight significant 
challenges for school -age children, school infrastructure, and learning environments, with shared themes of extreme 
weather events, rising temperatures, and rainfall variability posing threats to education.  
 
19. In South Sudan , increasing rainfall extremes and heightened flood risks will likely disrupt school attendance and damage 
educational infrastructure. Regions prone to flooding, such as central and northern areas, may experience repeated 
closures or unsafe conditions, limiting consistent access to education. Rising temperatures, with days exceeding 35°C 
and extended heat waves, will exacerbate health issues like dehydration and heat stress for children, impacting their ability to focus in classrooms that ofte
n lack adequate cooling. Prolonged heat and food insecurity from disrupted 
agriculture will further affect children’s cognitive performance and overall well -being.  
 
20. Cambodia  faces similar challenges, with intensified monsoons and increased frequency of extreme rainfall events leading 
to higher flood risks, especially in southern and northeastern regions. Flooding could damage schools, disrupt access, and pose sanitation chall enges, increasing the risk of waterborne diseases. Rising temperatures, with an average increase 
of 0.28°C per decade and a dramatic rise in days exceeding 35°C, will create challenging learning environments. Heat stress and lack of proper cooling systems will affect students’ focus and attendance. Droughts and shifting rainfall patterns 
during critical agricultural periods could strain food security, affecting children’s nutrition and learning capabilities, 
especially in rural areas.  
 
21. In Tonga , extreme rainfall events and tropical cyclones will continue to be significant threats to school infrastructure, with 
damage to buildings and disruptions to access during storms. While rainfall variability may lead to localized droughts, the 
primary conce rn is the rising temperatures, with extended warm spells increasing risks of heat -related illnesses and   22.5  23.0  23.5  24.0  24.5  25.0  25.5  26.0  26.5  27.0

13 World Vision (2019. Unlocking Cambodia’s Future: Improving Access to Quality Basic Education. Available here discomfort in learning environments. Increased reliance on cooling measures will strain resources, particularly in remote 
areas with limited access to electricity. The heightened frequency of extreme weather events will also add stress to 
families, potenti ally leading to higher absenteeism and school dropouts.  
  22. Across all three countries, climate- induced disruptions to school infrastructure, health, and family livelihoods will affect 
learning continuity and quality. Shared vulnerabilities include flooding and extreme heat, which damage facilities, disrupt access, and create unhealthy or uncomfortable conditions for children. For rural areas, where children often walk long distances to school, these impacts are magnified. Food and water insecurity resulting from climate disruptions add burden, 
impairing children’s physical and cognitive development . Such links between the climate and non- climate drivers, hazards  
and impacts are shown in the Table below . There are a multitude of climate impacts related to specific country contexts 
and their hazards  provided in the table, with some specific examples well -evidenced e.g.: flood impacts in Cambodia 
leading to longer commute times for children and thereby reducing hours of learning. It’s also important to acknowledge 
that while there may be differences country -to country, some hazards result in a common set of impacts as far as our 
understanding and scientific evidence shows when it comes to climate affected education . In paragraph 1 we describe 
these as: children dropping out of school, children and teachers absent from school, learning loss and impacts on learning 
outcomes, and gender -related impacts (e.g., increases in gender -based violence), and physical impacts that climate 
hazar ds have on school facilities (e.g. water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH) impacts, etc .). These climate- education 
impacts are broadly agreed and acknowledged and often referred to in global  documentation
5. The most recent  collection 
and presentation of what data is currently available on the climate change impacts on the education sector can be found 
in the World Bank report ‘The Impact of climate change on education and what to do about it’ April 2024, which can be 
found here. This includes refence to Tonga “The duration of school closures is prolonged when school infrastructure is 
vulnerable or when schools are used as evacuation centers. For example, between 50 to 90 percent of 6,000 school 
buildings across Samoa, Tonga, and Vanuatu may not withstand a strong cyclone.” These climate- education impacts are 
broadly agreed and acknowledged and often referred to in globa l documentation .6 On the other hand, very downscaled 
documented scientific evidence climate education impacts can be challenging to find, with often a prevalence of qualitative 
evidence gained through lived experiences and feedback from teachers, children and M inistry of E ducation stakeholders 
during field meetings and consultations ; though  some of these testimonies and lived experiences are outlined in the in 
the recently published report by the Geneva Global Hub for Education in Emergencies: ‘Leveraging Education in 
Emergencies for Climate Action’ , which can be found here. Please also see Annex 2: Feasibility Study sections 2.2 
(Cambodia), 3.2 (South Sudan) and 4.2 (Tonga).

23. BRACE offers a holistic solution to the climate vulnerabilities faced by the education sector; it is a first and has an 
ambitious goal. Given the uniqueness  of BRACE, there are no predecessors in terms of like- for-like initiatives .  Existing 
projects have addressed narrow aspects of climate change resilience in the education sector but have been limited in 
conceptual scope and/or geographical coverage. For example, in Cambodia, initiatives such as Green Generation and 
the Local Life Skills Education (LLSE) project have focu sed on climate change and environmental teaching and education 
and equipping students with the skills to solve local climate- related challenges. South Sudan has faced chronic 
underinvestment in climate- resilient educ ation, with interventions focused on humanitarian needs (Education in 
Emergencies) and disaster risk reduction, such as those supported by the Education Cannot Wait (ECW) Multi -Year 
Resilience Programme and NORAD Framework Agreement. In Tonga, the Safe and Resilient Schools Project (SRSP) is 
working in selected locations and educational programmes to enhance the safety and resilience of education facilities and 
improve the quality of education management, curricula and assessments  and BRACE spec ifically builds on the targeting


---

 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 16 
 
16 
  
84 N.B. As a landlocked country, South Sudan is not directly affected by sea- level rise.  of this project extending the reach of  SRSP . BRACE is designed to complement and  collaborate with synergistic education 
and climate initiatives , building on any foundational achievements from past/current programming in the three countries 
to fill gaps in  climate action in education and deliver a holistic solution  at scale to maximise impact.   Further details on 
complementarity with existing initiatives is provided in Annex 2: Feasibility Study (Sections 2.1.3, 3.1.4, and 4.1.5).  
 
24. Without the BRACE intervention, the ‘business -as-usual’ scenario will ensure targeted countries remain ill-equipped to 
cope with and adapt to current and projected climate change and extreme weather events, resulting in increased climate 
vulnerability, school closures, children dropping out of school, gender inequality, and learning loss es. Furthermore, 
Ministries of Education will not have the necessary resources to prioritise and access climate finance, nor develop 
national -level and realistic climate- related education policies, targets and programming to support strengthened resilience 
at this critical time. However, with the BRACE intervention in place, targeted countries and wider global education and 
climate networks will  be engaged to support  implement ation of  evidence- based climate adaptation actions that support 
school communities fighting exacerbated climate change, secur e a more optimistic and productive future for school 
children and their learning and development opportunities, and increas e the  resilience  of school assets.  
B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram  (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)  
25. The project’s paradigm shift goal statement is:  
 
IF the education stakeholders in climate- vulnerable countries are better informed of climate risks and adaptation options 
for the  education sector, as well as have access to global knowledge, policy exchange  and financing, and IF the target 
schools are teaching children about climate change and become safer and greener, THEN  the school systems of the 
target countries and beyond, including children and communities , become more resilient, BECAUSE , policies of the 
education sector becomes more responsive to climate change,  there will be increased climate finance for the sector, 
Ministry of Education staff, students and communit ies will become more knowledgeable and engaged in climate 
adaptation ac tions  and advocacy . 
 
26. This goal will be achieved via implementation of activities under three  components, each with an associated outcome: 
 
• Component 1: Building Climate- Resilient School Infrastructure and Systems with outputs and activities leading to 
Outcome 1  – The education sector at national and sub- national levels  in targeted countries is more resilient to the 
impacts of climate change . Sub-activities  under this component are  designed to address country -specific nuances in  
climate change vulnerability and the barriers faced.  
• Component 2: Enhancing Access to Climate Finance for Education , with outputs and activities leading to Outcome 2  
–  Education ministries of the target countries have improved access to climate finance to increase the resilience of 
the education sector to climate change Sub-activities under this component address  vulnerabilities and barriers 
common to all three project countries.  
• Component 3: Coordination and Knowledge Sharing on Climate and Education , with outputs and activities leading to 
Outcome 3  – Education and climate stakeholders are connecting, coordinating, collaborating and cross -learning for 
enhanced climate action in the education sector . Sub-activities under this component address vulnerabilities and 
barriers common to all three project countries.  
 
27. BRACE  causal links are indicated in the ToC figure below . These causal links represent  a dynamic relationship between 
the activities that directly address  the identified  barriers , enabling the climate risks to be reduced. This relationship al lows 
for the output results to be achieved, feeding into the higher -level outcomes , while delivering co- benefits across the 
education sector  and ultimately delivering the paradigm shift  ambition.  The project is designed to address multiple climate -
related hazards (including heatwaves, flooding, extreme rainfall, drought, and sea- level rise) and barriers to climate 
change adaptation in the education sector that, while nuanced, are generally common to all three countries.84 Component 
1 is designed to address these nuances through country -specific sub -activities, which are commonly clustered at the 
output/activity level; whereas Components 2 and 3 comprise sub- activities common across all three countries.  Project 
Outputs under each component will be mutually supportive with Output 1.1 on enabling systems and policies  supporting 
and informing action under Output 1.2  on safer and greener school facilities , Output 1.3 on school safety and educational 
continuity management systems, and Output 1.4 on embedding climate change resilience teaching and learnin g. These 
Outputs, delivered through country -specific interventions , will collectively address the full range of  barriers outlined (i.e., 
governance, social,  informational,  financial, technological, institutional , and gender and social inclusion) . Also causally 
linked is  Output 2.1  on climate risk -informed decision- making and influencing, which targets information and institutional 
barrier , and supports  achievement of Output 1.1  (especially  Activities 1.1.1 -1.1.3 on cross- sectoral coordination and 
integration of climate change into education  sector policies and plans ). Output 3.1  which establishes a  climate  resilient


---
*Page 23*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 17 
 
17 
 education co -investment platform  and Output 3.2 on peer learning and exchange  will serve as enablers to enhance  
achievement of Output 2.1  on climate risk -informed  decision -making and influencing, Output 2.2 on finance for climate 
action in the education sector , and Output 1.1  on enabling systems and policies.  In addition, all four Outputs under 
Outcome 1  on resilience of the education sector at national and sub-national levels  will enhance the optimisation of 
Output 3. 2 on peer learning and exchange. In particular, this will be achieved through the evidencing of interventions , 
which in turn allows for new knowledge on what works and where the gaps remain on the ground based on different 
contexts  and climate hazards. Output 2.2 and Output 3.1 will primarily address governance, financial and institutional  
barriers;  while Output 3.2  will primarily address information, technological  and institutional  barriers . This project logic 
demonstrates the links between outputs and outcomes  and the change required in the enabling environment through 
reducing the barriers to operate a strengthened climate r esilient  education sector.  
 
28. The project will also have three co-benefits  (see Section D.3 for  more detail) , including:  
 
• Co-benefit 1 : Gender  equity and social inclusion increases  in the education sector. This co- benefit will be 
achieved via activities that contribute to Outcome 1.  
• Co-benefit 2  (Environmental): Clean and green environment promoted in the targeted schools . School greening 
through reduced emissions from  school infrastructure and Natural Resource Management (NRM)  initiatives 
contributing to school -ground shading, windbreaking and cooling effects . This co -benefit will be achieved via 
activities that contribute to Outcome 1 (particularly Output 1.2).  
• Co-benefit 3  (Social ): Increased Locally- led Adaptation (LLA) – Influencing the LLA  agenda working at the local 
school level with children, resident teachers and parents/community members. This co -benefit is achieved through 
Outcomes 1, 2 and 3.  
 
29. The project will work to address a range of key barriers to scaling up climate action in the education sector in the target 
countries , as well as more broadly through Component 2 (Enhancing access to climate finance for education ) and 
Component 3 ( Coordination and knowledge sharing). An overview of how the project interventions are designed to 
overcome these barriers is provided in the  Table below . These underlying barriers limit the options available to the 
education sector in responding to climate change and extreme events. In addition, it is likely that unmitigated barriers increase cases of maladaptation practices as a short -term cost -effective coping mechanism. This is something clearly  
that the project  would both address and seeks to avoid.

designed in partnership with relevant  ministries to enable them  to overcome the current 
challenges preventing them from building an enabling environment that allows for more 
effective integration of  climate change into the education sector.

seeks to link MoEs  with those  responsible for climate change and disaster risk 
reduction ( i.e., national disaster management agencies ) and meteorological

B.3. Projec t/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)  
33. BRACE  focuses on enhancing the climate resilience of the education sector and addressing risks and impacts from 
droughts, heat stress , and floods . In Component 1 BRACE will be implemented in Cambodia, South Sudan, and Tonga 
combining climate- resilient interventions at national, sub- national, and school level s. Component 2 will work directly with 
Ministries of Education in the three target countries to strengthen their access to climate finance through the preparation 
of easy -to-use guidance and engagement in National Adaptation Planning processes . Component 3 will strengthen 
coordination on climate change and education with a focus on financing to support sustainability of the interventions in 
Cambodia, South Sudan, and Tonga. BRACE will directly benefit 60 7,098 people in Component 1, including  392,185  
people (188,373 female) in Cambodia, 201,242 ( 104,929 female) in South Sudan, and 13, 671 (6,631 female in Tonga). 
The nature of the work in Component 2 and Component 3 means the direct reach will be lower as the project will support  
and work with a select group of ministry offic ials, their partners and international organizations,  but the indirect reach,  will 
cover all school -aged children in the three target countries of BRACE . Therefore, it is estimated that Component 2 will 
directly benefit 210 beneficiaries and Component 3 will benefit 1,000 beneficiaries . The project will achieve climate-
resilient  education systems and support children’s learning through a multi -pronged approach as set out in the project 
ToC outlined in section B.2 . More information on the outcomes, outputs, and activities can be found in the feasibility study 
(Annex 2). Furthermore, t he BRACE project will be complemented by parallel project  finance from the Global Partnership 
for Education (GPE)  and with UNE SCO will help to address border  drivers of the challenges that children face in being 
educated in climate stressed environments , such as absenteeism and dropping- out, as the  GPE and UNESCO support 
government s broader system strengthening initiatives of the education systems  which  includes  tackling  the broader 
causes of absenteeism and dropping- out. 
 
34. Component 1  
OUTCOME 1: The education sector at national and sub -national levels in targeted countries is more resilient to the 
impacts of climate change.  
The activities under this outcome will enable policies to be in place to support climate -resilient education to ensure that school 
facilities are safer and more climate resilient , that school safety systems are in place and educational continuity is protected, 
and that climate- resilient teaching and learning is supported in schools. These activities will address governance, social, 
informational, financial, technological, institutional, and gender and social inclusion barriers.  
 
35. Output 1.1 Enabling systems and policies are in place and supported  
The activities under this output will support  Ministries of Education to incorporate climate change within their education sector  
plans and policies  and to strengthen  internal coordination with in the Ministry of Education on climate change and  cross -sectoral


---
*Page 27*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 21 
 
21 
 coordination with other line ministries . Girls and boys, including those with disabilities,  will participate in policy dialogues on 
climate change and education so that their priorities are heard in policy discussions.  
  
36. Activity 1.1.1 Facilitate preparation and endorsement of climate and education policies, plans, and guidelines  
This activity will work with Ministries of Education and the Environment, a nd other stakeholders to prepare national climate 
change and education plans, policies , and guidelines based on the prioritie s identified in each country. In Cambodia , BRACE 
will support the MoEYS to establish a National Climate Resilience in Education Framework , along with annual reviews of 
climate -related education budget allocations.  In South Sudan , BRACE will work with MoGEI to develop a climate change and 
education policy with implementation guidelines and update the Inter -Agency Network for Education in Emergencies (INEE) 
Minimum Standards (MS) to include climate change and the environment .  
 
37. Activity  1.1.2 Strengthen coordination on climate change within  Ministries of Education and other relevant  line 
ministries  at national and sub -national level    
This activity will support the Ministry of Education to have stronger internal and cross -sectoral coordination with Ministries of 
Environment and other line ministries responsible for climate change and DRR. In Cambodia , BRACE will organize annual 
multi -stakeholder dialogue events on climate change within the education sector . In South Sudan , BRACE will strengthen 
coordination between education and environment government officials at sub- national level , including the identification of 
climate change focal points , building o n the work initiated through CSESI .  
 
38. Activity  1.1.3 Facilitate integration  of Disaster Risk Reduction ( DRR)  into existing and/or new  plans with 
education authorities  
Education authorities at national and sub- national level in Cambodia and South Sudan will be supported to prepare and test 
through simulations their own DRR  plans. These plans will support education authorities to have procedures in place to 
minimise the impact of future climate- related emergencies  on the education sector .  
 39. Activity 1.1.4 Support children to participate in climate change and education policy initiatives  
Girls and boys, including those with disabilities, will be supported to participate in climate change and education policy 
dialogues . In Cambodia , BRACE will develop a moderated virtual web -based platform that will serve as a hub for young 
people to connect, and c hildren will also be supported to engage in policy dialogues with MoEYS on climate change. In South 
Sudan , children will participate in child parliaments – an existing structure in South Sudan - to champion climate change in 
education policies  through regular dialogues with MoGEI.  
 
40. Output 1.2 School facilities are safer and greener  
The activities under this output will focus on ensuring that school facilities are safer and climate- resilient for children, including 
boys and girls and those with disabilities, as well as educational personnel (teachers, school staff ), and School Management 
Committees (SMCs), as well as caregivers.   
  
41. Activity 1.2.1 Develop a national standard for climate -resilient school facilities  
Global guidance exists on how to strengthen the climate resilience of school infrastructure, including classrooms, and WASH 
facilities, but this guidance has not always been developed at country level. In Cambodia , BRACE will work with the MoEYS  
to upgrade their school design standards and construction guidelines to incorporate climate change and the environment . In 
South Sudan , BRACE will use the climate change and environment review of MoGEI’s Basic Construction Standards and 
Guidelines conducted as part of CSESI . In Tonga , BRACE will utilise the existing World Bank infrastructure assessments.  
 42. Activity 1.2.2 Retrofit school facilities , including WASH  and internet connectivity , to strengthen climate resilience 
This activity will apply the country -level standards developed in Activity 1.2.1 to strengthen the climate resilience of school 
facilities in Cambodia,  South Sudan,  Tonga through retrofitting. Physical interventions will be in line with Save the Children 
Australia’s ESS Category C Accreditation and be gender -responsive and disability inclusive. The infrastructure planning to 
address  the needs of each school targeted by BRACE will be led by suitably qualified technical experts . This will result in an  
infrastructure plan for each school  that will be integrated into the School Improvement Plan (SIP) . The school retrofits are 
anticipated to include, for example, cooling systems (Cambodia, Tonga) and improved ventilation (South Sudan), solar  
photovoltaic ( PV) systems to improve access to renewable and reliable energy, lighting, and internet connectivity (Tonga), 
providing shade through tree planting within school grounds (South Sudan) , rainwater harvesting and upgrading of drinking 
water systems and handwashing stations and latrines . In Cambodia and South Sudan , to prevent outbreaks of waterborne 
diseases that keep children out of school, awareness -raising sessions for students will be organized to promote handwashing 
and hygiene. In South Sudan, girls will receive  sessions on menstrual health and hygiene.  
 43. Activity
 1.2.3 Strengthen school communities and education officials to operate and maintain school 
infrastructure and facilities


---
*Page 28*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 22 
 
22 
 As part of the retrofit and upgrading of school facilities, school communities , including education officials (Cambodia, South 
Sudan , and Tonga ) will be trained in operations and maintenance of the school facilities. Joint monitoring and supervision 
will also be conducted by BRACE project staff and government officials throughout the project lifecycle.  
 
44. Output 1.3 School safety and educational continuity management systems are operating effectively  
The activities under this output will focus on ensuring that school safety management and educational continuity systems are 
in place. This will be done through building the capacity of education officials to provide training and on- going support to 
teachers, SMCs , Parent Teacher Associations (PTAs) and children on how to develop and implement climate -related school 
safety plans and  link those plans to overall School Improvement Plans (SIPs) . Climate resilient kits and equipment  will also 
be distributed, and access to Early Warning Systems (EWS) and Climate Information Services (CIS)  strengthened.  
 
45. Activity 1.3.1 Build capacity of national and sub -national  education officials to be master trainers on climate -
related school safety   
This activity uses a “Train the Trainer (ToT)” approach to build the capacity of education officials  at national and/or subnational 
level to be master trainers in climate- related school safety. Education officials selected as master trainers  for the BRACE 
project and beyond will be identified in partnership with the Ministry of Education in each country .  
 46. Activity 1.3.2 Build knowledge and capacity of school management, teachers,  and children to prepare climate -
related school safety plans    
Following the implementation of Activity 1.3.1, SMCs /PTAs , and teachers and children will be trained by education officials in 
how to implement school safety  and educational continuity . In South Sudan, traditional and religious leaders will also be 
involved as they play a key role in the wider school community. Refresher training and on- going support will be provided 
throughout the implementation of BRACE.  
 
47. Activity 1.3.3 Implement  school -level climate- related school safety plans.  
In this activity, SMCs, teachers and children from target schools trained in Activity 1.3.2 will be supported to implement what 
they have learned. The approach will depend on the guidance in each country but will broadly include  undertaking a gender -
inclusive climate risk assessment of the school  and preparing a climate- related school safety  plan. The plans will also be 
implemented by, for example, practicing drills, reaching out to the wider community  to share climate resilience knowledge and 
information, and integrating the school safety plan into the overall SIP, and in the case of Cambodia linking with commune investment and community DRR plans. Plans will be updated annually.  
 
48. Activity 1.3.4 Provide climate resilience tools, equipment and kits to schools.  
Schools will be provided with climate resilience kits specific to the needs in each context . It is anticipated these kits will include, 
for example,  buckets for sand to put out fires, whistles for guiding emergency drills in the school, flags for early warning, and 
other equipment such as megaphones, as required.  
 
49. Activity 1.3. 5 Strengthen access to and use of Early Warning Systems (EWS) and Climate Information Systems 
(CIS) in schools.   
This activity will focus on improving the availability of climate and early warning information to schools.  In Cambodia, BRACE 
will support school -level EWS that link to community EWS, and the development of a web- based dashboard that will integrate 
various data sources and provide visual aids including maps to support decision making by MoEYS.  In South Sudan , BRACE 
will establish and maintain a school -based early warning network system by linking trained SMCs/PTAs outlined in Activity 
1.3.2 and sharing early warning information on extreme events including floods, droughts and heatwaves  via multiple channels 
including SMS, radio stations, and broadcas ts. In full collaboration with the MEIDECC in Tonga, EWS and CIS products and 
materials will be developed and distributed to schools to ensure they are reached by EWS. EWS and CIS m odules will also 
be uploaded onto the government e-learning platform.  
 50. Output 1.4: Climate  change resilience teaching and learning  embedded in national systems  
The activities under this output will support the development and distribution of climate change educational materials for 
schools in Cambodia, South Sudan, and Tonga. It will also strengthen teachers'  capacity to deliver climate change lessons 
through training and mentorship and curriculum engagement . Child clubs, involving girls and boys, including those with 
disabilities will be engaged to lead climate- resilience initiatives in their schools and communities. The project will also use 
digital platforms for wide r dissemination of climate change information .  
 
51. Activity 1.4.1  Develop  teaching and learning materials on climate change ready to implement through national, 
non-formal , and informal curriculum  
This activity will support the development, printing,  and distribution of teaching and learning materials for children and teachers 
on climate change for use in school. In Cambodia , BRACE will utlitise the existing green generation approach endorsed for 
nationwide scale -up by the MoEYS  and as required, develop additional materials on topics such as green skills, child


---
*Page 29*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 23 
 
23 
 protection , and psychosocial first aid.  In South Sudan , BRACE will conduct a review of the climate change teaching and 
learning materials developed through CSESI  and develop and distribute additional materials, as required. Out-of-school 
children and children in pastoral  communities will be reached through mobile units  that disseminate materials to children. In 
Tonga , BRACE will develop an elective climate change subject for integration into the curriculum for the final two years of 
secondary school . This will include undertaking labour market research to understand current and future climate- related needs  
in the workforce.  
 
52. Activity 1.4.2 Build knowledge and capacity of teachers and education managers to implement climate change 
lessons inside the classroom  
Teacher s and education managers  in Cambodia, South Sudan, and Tonga will be trained and provided with on- going 
mentoring and coaching support to deliver climate change lessons inside the classroom. Before the delivery of any training, a 
training needs assessment will be undertaken. In Cambodia, teachers  will be trained in how to use the green generation 
materials  and incorporate green generation into routine classroom instruction, along with ongoing mentoring and coaching 
support . In South Sudan , BRACE will conduct a needs assessment to determine the most relevant training content in the 
target schools, and work with relevant bodies to develop and validate a teacher training plan. Teacher t raining will then be 
rolled out and aligned to the World Bank Building Skills for Human Development in South Sudan programme. In Tonga , 
teacher training on the climate change elective subject will be rolled  out, along with a review and updating of the climate 
change materials.  
 
53. Activity 1.4.3 Strengthen capacity of child clubs to lead climate change initiatives in their school and community  
Child clubs involving both girls and boys , including  children with disabilities , provide an ideal space for children to practice 
what they have learned about climate change in their homes and wider community . In Cambodia , BRACE will work  with 
existing school structures, including school councils and child clubs that currently focus on literacy and numeracy, and  expand 
their scope to encompass climate change. Child club members will be trained and supported to implement their activities and 
run environmental campaigns. In South Sudan , BRACE will work with existing or establish new  child clubs focused on climate 
change and the environment to encourage children to participate in climate- resilient actions.  
 54. 1.4.4 Disseminate climate change teaching and learning materials through Ministry of Education e -learning and 
other platforms  
Each country will upload the climate change materials to existing government e-learning platforms or other platforms. In 
Cambodia , BRACE will establish a virtual community of practice of green generation teacher facilitators  to facilitate future 
peer-to-peer support and learning. The green generation materials will also be integrated into MoEYS online platform for other 
teachers to access. In South Sudan , BRACE will share climate change teaching and learning materials through MoGEI’s 
radio platform. In Tonga , the traini ng for climate resilience focal points, as well as the climate change elective subject will be 
uploaded to the government e-learning platform.  
 
55. 1.4.5 Prepare learning documents and hold events with Ministries of Education and other partners to share 
experience of integrating climate change into the curriculum   
National curriculum revisions provide an opportunity to integrate climate change into different subjects , as is the case in Tonga. 
However,  Cambodia and South Sudan will not undertake curriculum revisions during the project timeframe so BRACE will 
instead document experience and lessons learned of integrating climate change and environmental education in classroom 
activities to share with Ministry of Education and other actors.   
56. Component 2   
OUTCOME 2: Education ministries of the target countries have improved access to climate finance to increase the 
resilience of the education sector to climate change 
The activities in this outcome will work with key stakeholders at national level – especially Ministries of Education, Direct 
Access Entities, and government departments responsible for national adaptation planning to strengthen access to climate 
finance and to contribute to national adaptation capacity. This outcome will focus on addressing the institutional and financial barriers outlined in the feasibility study (annex 2).  
 
57. Output  2.1 Key stakeholders (in BRACE  countries) have access to, and the ability to interpret and utilize relevant 
climate information as well as participate in decision -making and influence national adaptation planning 
processes including within the education sector    
The activities in this output will  address the financial and institutional barriers outlined in the feasibility study (annex  2) and 
support  Ministries of Education to access technical support to enhance their capacities to further  integrate climate change into 
policies and plans, including accessing future climate finance scaling up the BRACE project , that will ultimately allow education 
systems to more comprehensively adapt to climate change  therefore building a more resilient education system.

 58. Activity  2.1.1 : Development of education sector risk analyses on the impact of climate change and climate risks 
on the education sector and children’s learning   
Ministries of Education in Tonga, Cambodia and South Sudan will be supported with analysis that explores  the expected 
impact of climate change and environmental degradation on the education sector and children’s learning. This will include 
mapping and understanding the nature of climate risks , including through country -specific analysis of climate change impacts 
and overlaying these impacts on the education system in terms of access, quality , and education management as well as on 
different populations/groups of children, including girls.  Existing institutional, organizational , and individual education sector 
capacities for climate change action,  and identifying needs for additional capacities , will also be explored.   
 
59. Activity 2.1.2: Support Ministries of Education  to coordinate with climate change Ministries and engage in NAP 
processes, including those that may feed into global decisions such as those of the UNFCCC.  
This activity will support MoEs  in BRACE countries to advocate and ensure education is reflected in climate change adaptation 
plans at national and subnational levels, including in National Adaptation Plans (NAPs ), where possible.   This activity will 
involve providing training and capacity building support for Ministries of Education on UNFCCC processes and how to engage 
with Ministries responsible for climate change.  In addition, this activity will support a secondment of an education specialist to 
the NAP Global Network (GN). This secondment will support initiatives including conducting a thematic scan of NAPs for their 
inclusion of education,  children,  and youth as well as supporting national action on education, children,  and youth as requested 
by countries through the NAP GN .This scan will  help the BRACE countries  to use success from other countries  to inform how 
they integrate education in NAPs .. Cross-country peer -to-peer learning exercises for the three countries  will also be supported 
so that Ministries of Education, Environment, and F inance can share good practices and experience (linked to Activity 3.2.3 
which focus on south- south exchanges such as webinars and learning events ).  
 60. Output 2.2 Key stakeholders (in BRACE countries) have increased access to, and the ability to utilize, scaled up 
finance for climate action in the education sector  to replicate and scale BRACE to a greater percentage of the 
country  
Climate finance is increasing but Ministries of Education in the BRACE countries are not accessing their fair share. The 
activities in output 2.2 focus  on enabling education ministries to understand and access further climate finance to scale the 
projects and adapt to climate change and address the informational  and financial barriers  outlined in the feasibility study 
(annex 2).  
 
61. Activity 2.2.1: Development of climate finance mobilization road maps  
Through a better understating of the impacts of climate change on the education sector ( Activity 2.1.1), this activity will support 
MoEs to prepare forward looking  climate finance mobilization road maps  for beyond the end of BRACE . This will be done by 
determining the accessibility of all climate finance mechanisms in each country, mapping of co- financing and partnership 
opportunities, and development and dissemination of a climate finance mobilization road map for the education sector.  
 62. Activity 2.2.2:  Direct Access Entity (DAE) support for working with the education sector on GCF projects     
This activity will target D AEs and emerging DAEs  in the BRACE countries . It will focus on building the D AE’s engagement with 
Ministries of Education  to support them  to access climate finance. Activities will include socialization of the climate finance 
road maps  (Activity 2.2.1) , the development of education sector Simplified Approval Process ( SAP)  templates, the 
development of tools and guidance for DAE s to work with MoEs, and the provision of technical support to the DAE s to develop 
concept notes  focused on or including education for the GCF.  
 
Component 3  
63. OUTCOME  3: Education and climate stakeholders globally are connecting, coordinating, collaborating and cross -
learning for enhanced climate action in the education sector  
This outcome will strengthen collaboration between education and climate change actors  through the establishment of a 
BRACE climate and education financing coordination platform that will guide implementation of activities  to support the further 
scaling up of climate finance in BRACE countries . Peer learning and knowledge sharing will also be enhanced through the 
production of guidance materials on climate change and education financing, and the dissemination of those materials through 
peer learning events, and through strategic communication. The activities will address governance, informational, financial, 
technological, institutional, and gender and social inclusion barriers  outlined in the feasibility study (annex 2) . Lessons learned 
and success in C omponent 3 wil l be available to other  organisations  to replicate and scale  up, for example through the GPE 
parall el project financ e ‘Climate Smart Education Systems  Initiative ’ that will be reaching 35 countries  in total .

output, BRACE, in partnership with global climate change and education stakeholders, will establish a BRACE climate and 
education coordination platform, steering committee, and core team focused on climate financ e. This platform will contribute

65. Activity 3.1.1: Support a BRACE Climate and Education Financing Coordination Platform, including steering

BRACE will establish a climate and education coordination platform focused on financing to strengthen climate action in 
education . This platform will bring together global donors and implementers from climate change and education sectors to 
strengthen alignment of diverse investments in the climate/education nexus, and to bring education into climate change 
spaces . A voluntary  steering committee co- led by a high- profile climate actor  and a high-profile education actor will guide the 
activities of the platform. In addition, a core team will be established to deliver on the activities outlined below  and in Output 
3.2. Timebound working groups will be established, as required, to co- develop and deliver specific activities  that will directly 
benefit Cambodia, Tonga and South Sudan  and be prepared for replication and scaling up more broadly  through the CSESI 
parallel  project  financ e.  
 
66. Activity 3.1.2: Face- to-Face meetings between Ministries of Education and Environment for cross -learning and 
building political momentum  
This activity will focus on bring ing Ministries of Education, Environment , and Finance together for cross -learning and building 
political momentum for  climate change and education. This will involve  showcasing early successes in the BRACE countries 
to build moment um in those countries and in the future influence the GPE CSESI parallel  project  finance in a further 3 2 
countries . This will include supporting the implementation of the ‘ Declaration on the Common Agenda for Education and 
Climate Change ’ launched at the Conference of Parties ( COP ) 28 in Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga.  The activities will 
include fac e-to-face meetings at key moments, including COP 30 and beyond, identified by the steering committee outlined in 
Activity 3.1.1 above and will result in a roadm ap to support implementation of the Declaration.   
 
67. Activity 3.1.3 Map and track global climate finance opportunities in the education sector  
This activity involve s the mapping and tracking of global climate finance needs and opportunities from global climate funds 
(GCF, Adaptation Fund ( AF), Global  Environment Facility ( GEF)), bilateral donors, the private sector, philanthropies, etc. 
Findings will be translated  into easy to understand and use guides  with information by country that is updated regularly and 
housed on the BRACE coordination platform knowledge management site outlined in Activity 3.2.1 for easy access in Tonga, 
Cambodia and South Sudan  
 
68. Activity 3.1. 4: Support adjustments to international climate finance reporting to reflect dual -benefit investments 
in climate and education  
This activity will support the development of common methodologies to track the volume of education investments which 
include dual benefits for climate change adaptation and influenc e the Organization for Economic Co -operation and 
Development (OECD) Development Assistance Committee (DAC) climate finance tracking to better reflect the role of 
education in climate  change  designing and testing the methodologies in the BRACE countries to ensure they can be replicated 
and s caled more broadly.   
 
69. Output 3.2: Key stakeholders are supported to engage in peer learning and exchange through south -south 
collaboration   
This output will focus on ensuring that key stakeholders in Tonga, Cambodia and South Sudan from the global education and 
climate change communities can access and exchange knowledge and cross -learning on climate change and education.  This 
includes leveraging the CSESI  knowledge management platform, the development of guidance and knowledge products on 
topics focused on climate finance and education in Tonga, Cambodia and South Sudan, web inars, thematic sessions and 
learning events, and the development and dissemination of strategic communications materials. The activities will address 
informational, financial, and institutional barriers outlined in the feasibility study (annex 2).  
 
70. Activity 3.2.1: Develop and host information  on the CSESI knowledge  management platform on climate change 
and education for improved evidence and learning  
The GPE p arallel  project  financed  CSESI project will build an  education and climate change specific knowledge management 
platform while BRACE will support the development of information based on experience from the BRACE project  in the 3 
countries . This will enable cross country learning between BRACE countries and access to this information by the CSE SI 
countries  to further promote it s utilisation  in scaling up efforts on climate change  and education . This will build on and support 
the BRACE coordination platform outlined in Activity 3.1.1 and provide a space to engage donors to further support BRACE 
countries through disseminat ion of  guidance materials ( Activity 3.2.2), host ing webinars ( Activity 3.2.3) and maps (Activity 
3.1.3), and link to other climate change and education sites  and platforms .

 71. Activity  3.2.2: Develop guidance on climate and education for the GCF and the global education sector  
Based on the BRACE experience and lesson learned, with support and guidance from the steering committee (Activity 3.1.1) , 
this activity will support the GCF to develop an education sector guide, using the experience of implementation in the three 
BRACE focus countries . Additionally, BRACE will also produce a set of guidance documents for the BRACE countries  on 
climate finance, including a step- by-step guide on how to further  engage with and access climate funds from the GCF, and a 
knowledge product on how to engage in NAPs . These materials will be disseminated via the CSESI knowledge management 
platform ( see Activity 3.2.1), and webinars, thematic sessions and peer learning events (Activity 3.2.3).  This activity will 
contribute to a paradigm shift in scaling up of the BRACE interventions in the three focus countries  by sharing and 
disseminating guidance materials . This activity will also  have influence beyond the three focus countries of BRACE with the 
potential to reach a further 32 countries through the parallel project finance provided through the CSESI project.  
72. Activity 3.2.3: Organize webinars, thematic sessions and learning events on climate change and education  
To share learning and best practice amongst climate change and education stakeholders  in the three  countries , BRACE will 
organize webinars, and cross- country peer learning events workshops. The topics covered will include the guidance 
documents developed in Activity 3.2.2, as well as findings and lessons learned from the implementation of BRACE C omponent 
1 and 2, and other topics as identified throughout the course of the implementation of BRACE.  The events will be shared 
through the Knowledge Management platform  outlined in Activity 3.2.1 and other identified networks  and platforms . Up to 12 
webinars and peer learning event s will be conducted throughout  the project implementation period. The countries who are 
part of the CSESI parallel project finance will also be able to access these webinars and learning events thus sharing  the 
learning beyond the BRACE focus countries.  
 
73. Activity 3.2.4: Develop and share strategic communications materials  
Strategic communications materials will be developed and disseminated through the preparation of a communications plan 
and include social media outreach, op- eds and blog posts , case studies, lessons learned, and videos  to engage donors and 
partners to support the further scaling of activities in BRACE countries.  
 
B.4. Implementation arrangements (max.  1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams) 
74. As a multi -country project BRACE will have multiple Executing Entities (EEs). Each country ’s project implement ation will 
be considered as  separate, country -specific , and country -owned and each accorded an EE to manage fund flows with 
fiduciary requirement s in accordance with GCF standards. In Tonga , the EE is  a newly registered legal entity  with Save 
the Children  Tonga Trust  (SCTON) . In Cambodia the EE will be Save the Children International in Cambodia and the 
MoEYS . In South Sudan the EE will be Save the Children International in South Sudan .  SCTON, SCI South Sudan, and 
SCI Cambodia (jointly with MoEYS) will all sign Subsidiary Agreements (SAs)  with Save the Children Australia as the 
Accredited Entity (AE).   In South Sudan, the Ministry of General Education and Instruction (MoGEI) is an implementing 
partner, co- chairing the Project Steering Committee (PSC) and leading education sector coordination, policy alignment, 
and technical oversight. In Tonga, the Ministry of Education and Training (MET) serves as an implementing partner, co-
chairing the PSC and hosting the Project Implementation Unit (PIU), ensuring government leadership in planning and oversight. 
 MoGEI in South Sudan and MET in Tonga have no discretion in implementing the funded activities and in the 
usage of GCF Proceeds. The discretion will always remain with the SCISSD and SCTON, respectively .  In Cambodia, the 
Ministry of Education, Youth, and Sport (MoEYS) is a co- Executing Entity (EE) alongside Save the Children Cambodia, 
responsible for policy development, sectoral coordination, and technical leadership.  The co- financing agreement with 
GPE is secured as are the co- finance agreement with the World Bank and UNICEF  in Cambodia.  
75. Implementing partner UNESCO is a sole- sourced procured party for this project. UNESCO will have no discretion in 
implementing the funded activities and in the usage of GCF Proceeds. The discretion will always remain with the EEs.  
SCA will be the procuring entity, and the procurement will be done in accordance with the requirements under the AMA. UNESCO and its International Institute for Educational Planning (IIEP) have been closely supporting Education authorities in all countries  and leads the global Greening Education Partnership (GEP ) and provide technical assistance to ministries 
of education to address climate change through education policies, plans, curriculum and teacher professional 
development. UNESCO and Save the Children Australia are the implementing partners of the Global Partnership for 
Education ‘Climate Smart Education System Initiative’ (CSESI) and were selected by the GPE through an open 
competitive tender process of the GPE’s Grant Agents. The CSE SI project will provid e parallel project  finance for 
Component 2 and 3 of BRACE contributing to scaling up, sustainability and replication of good practices supported 
through BRACE .   
76. A high -level Project Steering Committee (PSC) will be established and responsible to provide strategic direction to 
Components 2 and 3 including providing directions to the overarching Project Implementation Unit (PIU).  The country  
PSCs will be co -chaired by Save the Children country offices and the government in each county. The AE will ensure that 
decisions taken by the PSC are consistent with the AMA, FAA , and FP by regularly holding meetings with the core project 
team, including the Project Team lead and the Country Director of SC country offices. This way, the AE will be updated 
on all key decision points, both during and ahead of time, so that they can inform the implementing partners of any risk of 
potential inconsistencies wi th the AMA, FAA , and FP. The AE will also run a learning and knowledge session during the


---
*Page 33*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 27 
 
27 
 project inception period on the AMA and the FAA so that all partners understand their responsibilities ahead of project 
implementation and understand what amendments may be need ed prior to AE and / or donor approval.  No financial flows 
will take place between Save the Children and the PSC s; the working relationship will be solely based on reporting and 
consultation.   
77. To meet the fiduciary requirements and standards of the GCF, legal and management oversight of the PIU s will be held 
by SC  Tonga, SCI Cambodia and SCI South Sudan in the three countries respectively . All have  the legal standing 
necessary to implement GCF activities in each of the three countries . They are also legally and organizationally bound to 
SCA, which acts as AE and holds the AMA with the GCF on behalf of the SC movement.   
78. Based on the due diligence assessment s of all EE in all three countries,  as AE, SCA will channel GCF resources to a 
project -specific bank account managed by the PIU in Tonga, Cambodia,  and South Sudan. The PIU s will use these 
resources and co- financing to manage project governance and implementation and will manage all downstream flows to 
the countr ies’ Ministries , implementing partner UNESCO , and any other procured partners. (Note: Implementing partners 
will be selected and subcontracted as procured parties in accordance with the AE’s procurement guidelines.)   
79. SCA will act as the international channelling executing entity for the project and will provide account management support 
to Save the Children Tonga (SCTON), Save the Children International Cambodia (SCIKHM)  and Save the Children 
International South Sudan (SCISSD) in accordance with the account management system employed by the Save the 
Children Association to ensure compliance and high- quality delivery of projects.  SCA will also act as the Executing Entity 
for Components 2 and 3. A small technical team within Save the Children’s Climate Change Division Technical Team will 
have responsibi lity for this work while AE oversight responsibilities will sit with the Division’s Operations Team . SCA have 
a long- term specialization (15  years) of working with Ministries of Education to integrate both DRR and Climate Change 
Adaptation (CCA) into the Education Sector policies, plans and programming. This has included the design and delivery 
of education sector DRR and climate change projects in Somaliland, Kenya, Timor Leste, the Philippines, Bangladesh, 
Vietnam, Cambodia, Laos, Vanuatu, Solomon Islands, Fiji , Tuvalu and Kiribati. For two years SCA held a partnership 
agreement with the Global Fund for Dis aster Risk Reduction  (GFDRR)  of the World Bank to support the development of  
their Global Program for School S afety. SCA had a staff member  seconded to the Association of Southeast Asian Nations 
(ASEAN ) secretariat  for several  years to support their leadership in regional school safety work. SCA are a founding 
member of the Global Alliance for Dis aster  Risk Reduction and Resilience  in the Education Sector (GADRRES) and lead 
author of the Comprehensive School Safety Framework. Globally , Save the Children is  the co- cluster lead of the Education 
in Emergencies (EiE) cluster and accreted to both Education Cannot Wait (ECW) and the Global Partnership for Education  
(GPE) .  
80. Tonga.  In Tonga,  SCTON will sign an implementing partner agreement with MET.  MET will enter into a Memorandum of 
Understanding (MoU) with other implementing partner ministries including the MEIDECC , departments within the Ministry 
of Health (responsible for WASH) , and Ministry of Internal Affairs (responsible for w omen’s affairs). Any other f ormal 
project agreements with other ministries will be managed through established government systems.  
81. The project  steering committee in Tonga will be co -chaired by Save the Children Tonga and MET. A Project Steering 
Committee and National Technical Working Group will guide and advise on implementation. Membership will be agreed 
with the Government and implementing ministries during inception and will include the EEs and MEIDECC .  
82. The Tonga PIU will manage and oversee delivery of the agreed project activities reporting into both Executing Entities 
(Save the Children Tonga and MET).  The P IU lead (Project Manager) will formally report to Save the Children Tonga as 
the designated Executing Entit y (EE) in Tonga. The Project Manager will also have accountability  to the Project Steering 
Committee (PSC)  to ensure that all key decisions are in alignment with the strategic direction provided by the PSC.  The 
PIU core team will be located in MET as the lead agency . The Operations Manager will lead finance, risk, human 
resources , procurement and general administration. A provincial project coordinator will work closely with the outer island 
government representatives to coordinate activity implementation in outer island locations .  
83. SCA has been operating in Tonga since 2021 . SCA’s  Board of Directors establish ed a newly registered legal entity in 
Tonga in 2024. With the establishment of a registered legal entity, SCA will establish a fully functional organisation similar 
to existing operations that currently implement GCF projects Vanuatu (FP184) and the Solomon Islands (SAP027) . Recent 
programming in Save the Children Tonga ( SCTON ) includes the Tonga Volcano Recovery Program (TARP II), which 
strengthens disaster risk resilience by enhancing access to educational materials through the Hama E -learning Platform 
(HeLP) including offline access for remote islands. IHunga Ha’apai Disaster  and COVID19 Recovery and Resilience  
Activation which establishe d a National School Safety and Resilience Coordination Committee, provides tools and training 
and implements safety self -assessments in 50 schools to inform School Development Plans. The P acific Coalition for the 
Advancement of School Safety ( PCASS)  project focuses on regional Safe Schools coordination, creating a Community 
of Practice, and implementing a regional monitoring mechanism aligned with Pacific frameworks. The GPE funded Climate 
Smart Education Systems Strategic Capability Initiative seeks to enhance countries’ capacities to mainstream climate 
change adaptation and environmental sustainability into education sector plans, budgets and strategies and to enhance 
education ministry capacity for cross -sectoral coordination on climate and environment -related policy and programming.  
84. Cambodia.   Save the Children Cambodia and MoEYS will sign a tripartite Subsidiary Agreement with Save the Children 
Australia as the AE.  In Cambodia, MOEYS will follow standard practice within the Government of Cambodia to establish 
relationships with the Ministry of the Environment and any other Government partners  that may be supporting the project .

 85. The project steering committee in Cambodia will be  co-chaired by Save the Children International Cambodia (SCIKHM)
