86. Save the Children Cambodia has extensive experience delivering climate resilience and education projects, positioning 
the organization as a key player in implementing programmes similar to BRACE. Notably, Save the Children led the Green 
Generation programme , a successful initiative integrating environmental education into formal and informal learning 
settings across Cambodia and other countries. This program me engaged children and communities to address local 
environmental challenges, with a focus on climat e change education, waste management, and water conservation. Its 
success was built on strong collaboration with local schools and government agencies, including MoEYS, and involved 
the development of teaching units and hands -on eco- projects in schools.  
87. Additionally, Save the Children Cambodia has contributed to school safety and disaster preparedness through its work 
under the ASEAN Safe Schools Initiative and the development of Cambodia’s School Safety Guidelines. These projects have equipped schools with DRR strategies, such as contingency planning and climate- proofing infrastructure, ensuring 
educational continuity during extreme weather events. Save the Children Cambodia
’s strong partnerships with the 
Cambodian government and grassroots organizations further highlight s its capability to scale up climate adaptation efforts, 
ensuring vulnerable children continue learning even in the face of environmental crises.  
88. South Sudan.  In South Sudan Save the Children will be the sole Executing Entity but will work in close collaboration and 
coordination with the Ministry of General Education and Instruction (MoGEI)  as an implementing partner .  SCI South 
Sudan will sign a partner agreement with MoGEI.  
89. The project  steering committee in South Sudan will be co- chaired by Save the Children International South Sudan 
(SCISSD)  and MoGEI and comprise the following ministries:  Ministry of Environment and Forestry , Ministry of W ater 
Resources and Irrigation, Ministry of Gender, Ministry of Ch ildren and Social Welfare , and Ministry of Disaster 
Management and Humanitarian Affairs. The purpose of the steering committee will be to provide overall guidance to the 
program me, and  make crucial decisions as a governance body.   
90. The South Sudan PIU will manage and oversee delivery of the agreed project activities reporting to SCISSD . The Project 
Manager  of the PIU will formally  report to SCISSD as the EE, while maintaining close coordination with MoGEI. The 
Project Manager will also have accountability  to the Project Steering Committee (PSC) ensuring alignment with overall 
project objectives  and governance frameworks.  The P IU core team will be hosted by SCISSD.   
91. Save the Children South Sudan is well positioned to deliver the BRACE program, having extensive experience in 
managing education initiatives, including the GPE Accelerated Funding floods response programme  integrated within the 
Education Cannot Wait (ECW)  Multi- Year Resilience Programme (MYRP -2023- 2026). With a n overall portfolio of over 
$90 million for this program, Save the Children has proven its capacity to work with diverse donors such as GPE, ECW, 
EU, UNICEF, and USAID, among others. These programmes are implemented in collaboration with key South Sudanese ministries like MoGEI and MGCSW, as well as a wide range of international and local partners, ensuring the effective 
reach and sustainability of its education interventions. The organi sation al so engages grassroots community -based 
organi sations to ensure that even the most marginalized children are reached.  
92. The collaboration between Save the Children and MoGEI under the GPE Accelerated Funding modality emphasizes 
shared responsibilities in planning, implementation, and monitoring, enhancing accountability and sustainability. This 
partnership strengthens MoGEI 's capacity to provide oversight and ensure the delivery of quality education services. Save 
the Children’s education programme  focuses on addressing the protection and developmental rights of vulnerable 
children, improving access to education, and support ing initiatives such as continuous teacher development, inclusive 
education for children with disabilities, and Education in Emergencies (EIE). Additionally, it builds climate- resilient 
education systems to ensure long- term sustainability.

B.5. Justification for GCF  funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)  
95. As announced at COP28 with the GCF, the GPE CSESI project will invest USD 21 million to support 35 countries to 
integrate climate change int o the education sector. This funding is essential to enable Least -Developed Country ( LDC) 
resource- poor Ministries of Education to undertake the necessary analysis of the impact and risks of climate change on 
the education sector in order to plan what they can and should be doing to protect education sector investment.   
96. Whilst  GPE funds, as parallel project finance to the BRACE project, will enable sector -based analysis and priorities 
planning, there is a gap in funds available for action at scale. Due to  no clear  financial return on investment , private sector 
financing is lacking to date.  However , the GCF’s ability to provide investment to de- risk the BRACE interventions, will 
demonstrate a strong investment case in the face of a changing climate  (see Annex 3 EFA). 
97. Funding a climate resilience program me in education aligns with several SDGs, particularly SDG 4 (Quality Education) 
and SDG 13 (Climate Action). It demonstrates a commitment to promoting quality education while addressing the urgent need for climate action in vulnerable settings.
 Education has been shown to contribute to economic resilience. By 
equipping young people with the skills and knowledge to adapt to climate change, the program me contributes to the 
development of a more resilient workforce capable of driving sustainable economic activi ties.   
98. Ministries  of Education in Cambodia, South Sudan, and Tonga and beyond in CSESI countries lack sufficient finance to 
integrate climate change into the education sector  (see Component  1). 
Component 1 
Cambodia  
99. Cambodia is highly vulnerable to climate change, facing recurrent natural hazards such as floods, droughts, and extreme weather events.  These climate impacts, as detailed in the National Action Plan for Disaster Risk Reduction (NAP DRR) 
2024-
2028, disrupt educational services, destroy infrastructure, and displace communities, compounding existing 
challenges in the education sector. Despit e being a low emitter of greenhouse gases, Cambodia is disproportionately


---
*Page 36*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 30 
 
30 
 affected by climate change. This creates an urgent need for targeted interventions that not only protect the current 
education system but also embed resilience measures to future- proof it against ongoing and future climate risks.   
100. Cambodia has limited access to financial resources to fund climate change mitigation and adaptation needs. Cambodia’s 
capacity to leverage domestic public and private sector funding to meet Nationally Determined Contributions ( NDC)  needs 
of US$5.8 billion for mitigation and US$2 billion for adaptation NDC needs largely depends on Cambodia’s ability to 
mobilize international funding from donors187.  The proportion of education expenditure as a share of total government 
expenditure has steadily declined over the past 4 years and recurrent expenditure on education remains below the 
recommended level of 20%.  
101. Currently, the Royal Government of Cambodia faces significant challenges in securing adequate funding and capacity to 
address the critical intersection of the education sector with climate risks and challenges. While there have been strides 
in education reform and expansion, as outlined in the Education Strategic Plan (ESP) 2024- 2028, the integration of climate 
resilience measures within the education system remains insufficient. Both public and private sector investment in climate 
resilience in education i s minimal, and existing donor funding prioritizes basic education without ensuring that education 
systems are equipped to withstand and adapt to climate impacts.  
102. Investing in climate resilience within the education sector in Cambodia is essential not only for safeguarding educational 
progress but also for promoting sustainable community practices and preparing future generations to address the 
challenges of climate change. This investment will not only address immediate educational needs but will also contribute 
to long- term national resilience, as Cambodia navigates the dual challenges of development and climate vulnerability.  
 
South Sudan  
103. The Government of South Sudan does not currently have sufficient funding and required capacity to address the urgent 
needs of the education sector in relation to climate risks and challenges. B udget  allocation to education is currently low, 
with geographic disparities in access to education resources. Despite the education budget increase over the past three 
fiscal years, the government’s severe under -funding of its education system leaves it  inadequate to achieve universal 
quality primary education and support system transformation.  Current donor funding focus es on basic education support 
to ensure children go to school and stay in school without any necessary support to ensure climate resilience of the 
education system, nor school children’s saf ety when facing extreme weather events .  
104. South Sudan is  aligned to GCF geographic priorities as it is an LDC facing severe climate change risks and is highly 
vulnerable , despite negligible Greenh ouse Gas (GHG) emissions . Climate change result s in extreme weather events, 
including floods and droughts  that disrupt educational services, displace populations, and exacerbate existing challenges 
within the education sector, creating an urgent need for a climate resilience programme .  
105. Funding a climate resilience program me under the education sector in South Sudan is crucial for safeguarding education, 
promoting sustainable community practices, and preparing future generations to address the challenges of a changing 
climate. Such an investment not only addresses immediate educational needs but also contributes to long- term resilience 
and sustainable development in South Sudan.   
 
Tonga  
106. In Tonga,  the Government has been generally reliant on foreign funding and remittances (of a significant number of 
citizens who have left the country to work abroad),  which  contributed to nearly 45% of the total national budget.   The 
Government does not currently have sufficient funding or capacity to meet the immediate adaptation needs of the 
education sector  and schools do not have the resources to support required adaptation actions. Current  donor funding is 
not covering these needs , making it  necessary to ensure climate resilience of the education sector.  
107. Tonga is a SIDS , in alignment with GCF geographical priority areas, and has particular vulnerabilities to climate change. 
Tonga’s contribution to global GHG emissions is negligible, yet Tonga is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world to climate change due to its  geographic location, status as a SIDS , and the importance of natural resources to its main 
economic sectors of fisheries ”.
 125  
108. The risk of debt distress rating for Tonga is high.126 Tonga’s indebtedness  gradually declined from 2015 to 2019. However, 
the economy has been hit by multiple shocks: cyclone Gita in 2015, the Covid -19 pandemic, cyclone Harold in 2020 and 
the Hunga Tonga- Hunga Ha’apai volcanic eruption and tsunami in 2022. The budget deficit is expected to rise sharply in 
the coming years due to a gradual decrease in grants under the current commitments and growing spending needs for 
the SDGs and climate resili ence. Debt s ervice payments are spiking in 2024 and the following years due to scheduled 
repayment s to other countries ; 80% of Tonga’s upcoming debt payments will flow to other countries  as it owes other  
countr ies more than USD 120 million (approximately a quarter of Tonga’s GDP).127 The bulk of this debt must be repaid 
by 2028. These current repayment s will severely hamper Tonga’s recovery and crowd out much- needed funds for climate 
change adaptation. For example, the volcanic eruption and tsunami alone led to damages equivalent to 36% of GDP.  
109. The lack of global climate finance is a significant challenge for Tonga. It is apparent that SIDS’ status as representing a 
special need for climate resilient development “does not translate into effective prioritization or allocation of funds”.128 
Firstly, SIDS’ public sectors inherently face major human and technical capacity constraints to design and implement

85 Save the C hildren and G lobal Partnership for Education ( 2023), Climate -Smart Education Financing: A review of the evidence and new 
costing framework.  
86 Ibid  
87 Global Education Monitoring Report Team (2023) Global Education Monitoring Report, 2023: technology in education: a tool on w hose 
terms? Available at: Home - 2023 GEM Report (unesco.org)    climate projects. The complexity of the climate finance landscape and lack of harmonisation among the requirements of 
multilateral climate funds and donors further exacerbate this challenge. Secondly, the current climate and development 
finance systems fai l to adequately accommodate SIDS’ unique needs, realities and vulnerabilities, resulting in fewer 
funding opportunities for SIDS. Data limitation for adaptation projects, high transaction costs , and small project sizes make 
it difficult for SIDS to attract investment and compete for access to climate finance.  
110. While  adaptation initiative s have been developed in Tonga, climate change adaptation remains fundamentally 
underfunded. Even though the World Bank is currently implementing  the Tonga Safe and Resilient Schools Project  
(TSRSP) reaching approximately 62 schools with infrastructural retrofits and replacements, this not enough to cover the 
needs of 36,008 school -aged children ( age 4 to 18). The private sector is underdeveloped and faces difficulties in Tonga, 
especially in reaching the outer islands most vulnerable for climate change and cannot be relied on to build adaptive 
capacity.  
111. Difficulties  in accessing  climate finance, the current financial situation of Tonga, a nd the urgent need to strengthen 
climate resilience of Tonga’s education sector justifies the GCF funding request. Without such climate finance support, Tonga’s education model including the school children, their futures and the education sector and systems, will  fall further 
and possibly more rapidly into climate vulnerability with very little opportunity to cope or adapt to projected  climate change 
and extreme weather events.
 
 
Component 2  
112. According to the World Bank, education is a powerful but under -used instrument for climate action. Yet only 1.5 per cent 
of climate finance went to the education sector in 2021.  The CSESI project has held consultations with more than 20 
countries  and Ministries of Education consistently request ing support for  understanding and engaging in national climate 
change processes and accessing climate finance  – including Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga . There is currently no 
specific mechanism to provide support to Ministries of Education and Direct Access Entities to access climate finance. 
Most national efforts to support climate change adaptation or mitigation fo cus on agriculture, food security , energy , and 
transport  with little to no public funds being invest ed in the education sector  being enabled to access climate finance, 
other than GPE CSESI which is providing parallel project finance to this project . This is largely d ue to the education sector 
not being seen as an obvious investment  to tackle the climate cri sis85. An NDC Partnership assessment  of NDC shows 
that only  17 county NDC plans m ention Ministries of Education. Furthermore,  a recent study of multi -lateral  climate funds 
between 2006 and 2023 showed only one education sector - focused project out of 591 funded86. This highlights  the 
current barriers  the education sector faces in access ing climate finance. Component 2 of BRACE will focus on working 
directly with Ministries of Education and Direct Access Entities (DAEs)  in the three focus countries  of BRACE (Cambodia, 
South Sudan and Tonga), supporting them to access climate finance and addressing the market failure of ensuring climate 
finance reaches Ministries of Education through demand- driven and country -owned process es.  
 
Component 3   
113. Component 3 aims to directly support the activities of Component 2  by galvanising international support of donors and 
Ministry of Education partners in support  of country -level demand- driven climate change and education sector acti on. As 
noted above there is a current and chronic underinvestment by all financial instruments to support Ministries of E ducation 
to build the climate resilience of the education system. Ministries of Education in Africa, SIDS , and LDC s are rarely able 
to secure public financing  beyond minimum provision of  basic education services. UNESCO  estimate th at there is 
currently a USD 97 billion annual financing  gap to achieve national SDG4 Benchmarks  (Quality Education)87, and it is 
impossible for countries to allocate public finances to address the impact of climate change on the education sector  with 
competing needs .  Activities under C omponent 3 will specifically  address this gap by building an international platform for 
collaboration, coordination  and timely  financing.  As outlined in the F easibility Study ( Annex  2) all the organisations 
consulted (donors, multilateral banks, CSOs and network organisations ) unanimously highlighted that whilst there was 
great interest in coordination and collaboration on financing climate change investments  in the educ ation sector, there is  
no existing  platform that ha s the membership or man date to facilit ate these exchanges . The BRACE project  will kick start 
such a platform with the aim of integrating it into an exi sting mechanism for  long-term sustainability.  
 
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately  1 page)


---
*Page 38*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 32 
 
32 
 114. The BRACE project in South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga focuses on achieving climate resilience while ensuring long -
term sustainability and local ownership. The exit strategy aims to transition from project implementation to local 
stewardship, ensuring that established practices endure and evolve. This strategy emphasi ses scaling successful 
practices, integrating climate actions into national policies and budgets, and building institutional and community 
capacities.  
 
National Policy Alignment  
115. The project is aligned with national policies in each country to promote long- term sustainability. In South Sudan, BRACE 
supports the South Sudan Development Plan (SSDP) and the Revised National Development Strategy (R -NDS) 2021–
2024. It focuses on gender -responsive policies, safer and greener school facilities, and the development of robust school 
safety and educational continuity systems. These efforts are designed to make the education sector more resilient to 
climate change, ensuring minimal disruption to learning even during climate- related crises.  
116. In Cambodia, the project integrates with the Comprehensive School Safety Framework (CSSF) and the Child- Friendly 
School Program, enhancing education policies with a focus on climate- smart systems, environmental sustainability, and 
disaster risk management.  By establishing platforms for knowledge sharing and coordination, the project enables the 
MoEYS and other relevant ministries to integrate climate resilience into educational planning and access climate finance.  
117. In Tonga, BRACE aligns with the Tonga Strategic Development Framework 2015- 2025, the Joint National Action Plan for 
Climate Change Adaptation and Disaster Risk Management (JNAP II) 2018- 2028, and the Tonga Education Policy 
Framework. The project addresses key climate drivers such as sea level rise, temperature increases, and extreme 
weather by enhancing the physical resilience of school buildings and infrastructure. It mainstreams climate change into 
educational curricula and improves access to climate educ ation through e- learning platforms. These efforts ensure that 
the project supports Tonga’s strategic priorities, contributing to a sustainable and resilient education sector.  
118. Components 2 and 3 of BRACE align with the Declaration on the Common Agenda for Education and Climate Change, 
particularly the key area commitments of adapt and invest.  
 
Local ownership and capacity building  
119. Building local ownership and strengthening institutional capacity are critical to ensuring the sustainability of the project’ s 
outcomes. In South Sudan, BRACE enhances the capacity of the MoGEI by establishing robust systems and supportive policies that fa
cilitate climate change adaptation and resilience within the education sector. This includes training 
program mes in climate risk assessment, disaster risk management, and climate- resilient infrastructure planning. The 
project also involves appointing focal  points at national and sub- national levels to ensure coordination between climate 
change adaptation efforts and educational initiatives. These steps are crucial for ensuring that local institutions are well -
prepared to manage and scale climate adaptation initiatives, leading to sustainable project outcomes.  
120. In Cambodia, the project strengthens the capacity of MoEYS and other relevant ministries by establishing a platform for 
knowledge exchange and coordination. This platform facilitates the implementation of climate education and disaster risk 
management within the education sector, supported by the development of a National Climate Resilience in Education 
Framework. This framework will enable MoEYS to integrate climate actions into educational policies and access climate finance, ensuring the long- term sustai nability of the project’s outcomes.  Through CSESI, a consolidated framework  for 
school -based risk reduction and adaptation will be developed and tested with MoEYS. This framework will be implemented 
with and through education government officials and in schools through BRACE.  
121. In Tonga, BRACE builds the institutional capacity of the MET and MEIDECC  by providing training in CIS and EWS. The 
project also focuses on improving the physical resilience of schools through infrastructure upgrades and support to e-
learning and climate information dissemination. These efforts ensure that local institutions are equipped to manage and 
scale climate adaptation initiatives, contributing to sustainable project outcomes.  
 
Scaling best practices  
122. The project is designed to scale best practices across regions, ensuring maximum impact. In South Sudan, best practices 
in climate -resilient education, WASH management, and disaster preparedness will be scaled through knowledge- sharing 
platforms and the development of school -specific improvement plans. This includes initiatives such as the integration of 
climate resilience and disaster risk reduction education into the national curriculum, the establishment of green school 
initiatives, and the improvement of WASH facilities to support climate adaptation efforts.  
123. In Cambodia, the project scales successful models in climate- resilient education and school safety by retrofitting schools, 
enhancing WASH infrastructure, and rolling out the Green Generation initiative. This initiative integrates environmental education and resilience into school curricula and non- formal education activities, promoting environmental awareness. 
These efforts ensure that best practices in climate resilience and education are shared, adapted, and scaled to enhance 
sustainability across the education sector.  
124. In Tonga, the project builds on previous assessments and initiatives, such as the Outer Island Renewable Energy Project 
(OIREP) and the World Bank’s disaster risk assessment of school infrastructure. The project focuses on retrofitting and upgrading the most climate -
vulnerable schools, enhancing their resilience to extreme weather events, and ensuring 
inclusive WASH facilities. The integration of solar PV systems will improve energy efficiency and support Internet


---
*Page 39*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 33 
 
33 
  
 connectivity, which is crucial for accessing climate information services and early warning systems. The project also aims 
to increase access to climate education through the development of an elective climate change subject for secondary 
schools, linked t o the H ama e-learning platform. This approach ensures that best practices in infrastructure resilience, 
renewable energy integration, and climate education are scaled and adapted to the unique context of Tonga, enhancing 
the sustainability of project outcomes.  
125. Component 2 focuses on supporting Ministries of Education in the three BRACE countries to access climate finance and 
influence national climate change plans and policies. Best practices will be scaled up through templates that countries 
can use and adapt to access climate finance and through cross -country learning opportunities on climate finance.  
Component 3 will capture and disseminate best practices through a dedicated knowledge management platform, face- to-
face meetings, and through webinars that will provide a space to disseminate best practices and facilitate cross -country 
learning  across the three BRACE focus countries .  
 
Operations and Maintenance Strategy  
126. A robust operations and maintenance (O&M) plan is  vital to the sustainability of the project’s infrastructure and activities. 
In South Sudan, community -led maintenance committees will manage the upkeep of water systems and climate- resilient  
school buildings, supported by ongoing monitoring and supervision to maintain effectiveness. The project includes 
conducting comprehensive WASH and infrastructure assessments, developing tailored improvement plans, and 
implementing the Green School Initiat ives within school systems.   
127. In Cambodia, O&M plans will be established for retrofitted schools, ensuring gender and disability inclusivity. The project will provide training to school staff and local administration on maintaining school infrastructure, while linking these efforts 
to broader school safety management systems. Community -based monitoring systems will further ensure the continued 
functionality of these infrastructures, supported by local government plans and budgets.  
128. In Tonga, the O&M plan emphasizes the long- term functionality of climate- resilient school buildings and systems. The 
project delivers training on basic upkeep and maintenance of school infrastructure and WASH facilities to prevent common issues that undermine climate resilience, such as poor ventilation and sea spray corrosion. Additionally, the project will 
train school staff in problem- solving for Internet connectivity issues, ensuring the sustainability of the I
nternet and 
renewable energy systems instal led in schools. These efforts are designed to ensure that the infrastructure improvements 
remain effective and resilient over time, with local stakeholders equipped to manage ongoing maintenance.  
129. This exit strategy for BRACE in South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga is designed to ensure that the project’s benefits and practices are sustained and scaled beyond its duration. By focusing on transitioning to local ownership, scaling best 
practices, and inte grating climate actions into national policies, the BRACE project aims to create lasting impact and 
resilience in each country.  The exit strategy for Component 2 will ensure that Ministries of Education either have access 
to climate finance, or a roadmap i n place to support them to access climate finance by the end of the project.  This approach 
will ensure sustained results where Ministries of Education are leading efforts to build resilience and adapt to the impacts of climate change. 
In Component 3, the BRACE coordination platform will initially be dedicated to the project, but 
throughout the course of implementation,  the most appropriate place for the platform to be housed will be identified as a 
part of the exit strategy. This will ensure that  coordination efforts around the climate and education finance nexus continue 
after the end of the project. Furthermore, knowledge management assets will remain accessible to climate and education 
actors .

 EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the 
GCF’s Initial Investment Framework .  
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
 
131. Component 1. The project will make a contribution to GCF’s overarching adaptation impact , contributing to 
increased climate- resilient sustainable development by directly increasing the climate resilience of  people in 
Cambodia, Tonga and South Sudan. BRACE ’s total  direct beneficiary population is 607,098 (comprising 299,933 
females)  and total indirect beneficiary population is 8,640 ,844 (comprising 4,266,850 females ). BRACE will 
address climate impacts and risks in 850 schools.  The breakdown by countries under Component 1 is: 
 
132. Cambodia : At least  240 primary schools are selected as target schools in 3 provinces  of Kampong Chhang, 
Kampong Cham and Koh Kong where geographical risks are heightened and climate impacts on the education 
sector are significant, particularly due to the provinces’ vulnerability to extreme weather events, especially  flooding.  
Direct  beneficiaries include primary school children , their teachers, school principals, school management 
committees, parents, caregivers and other community members, education officials from the MoEYS, and Provinc e 
and District Office of Education, Youth and Sport, local administration office staff who receive capacity building 
training  and directly engage with our project activities.  A total of 392, 185 direct beneficiaries will be reached 
(188,373 female) amounting to 2 .3% of the population. A total of 4,031,400 indirect beneficiaries will be reached 
(1,962,128 female) amounting to 24% of the population.  
 
133. South  Sudan: C hildren within a total  of 510 schools  across the project, with an estimated total of 201,242 direct 
beneficiaries  (based on average school enrolments per school, and including additional beneficiaries from the 
wider communities). Of these, just over half ( 52%)  will be women and girls. BRACE in South Sudan will also impact 
an estimated 4,572,682 indirect beneficiaries  (50% female) , which is the total population of the six target counties, 
minus the direct project beneficiaries.  The direct beneficiaries comprise 1.8 % of the total population, and indirect 
beneficiaries  comprise 41% of the total population88 
 
134. Tonga : The climate resilience of 100 r emote schools will be strengthened to better cope with climate hazards such 
as sea level rise, droughts, floodings, extreme weather including tropical cyclones and heatwaves, and ongoing 
climate change. This includes 1 3,671 direct beneficiaries ( 6,631  femal e) equivalent to 13% of the total population; 
in addition to 36,762 indirect beneficiaries ( 18,381 female) equivalent to 33 % of the total population.  
 
135. Component 2  of BRACE will target Ministries of Education in the three BRACE focus countries supporting them 
to access climate finance and to lead climate- resilient sustainable development in the education sector. Direct 
beneficiaries will be  210 Ministry of Education and other government  officials from  the three focus countries , with 
the impact potential to reach children and education personnel in each country. This will be complemented by the 
CSESI project that works in  a total of  35 countries  by sharing learning from the implementation of BRACE project 
with other countries.  
 
136. Component 3  of BRACE will directly reach 1,000 , including the education and climate change actors and 
organisations in each of the three countries, including civil society. As with C omponent 2, the parallel  project  
financed CSESI project will support replication of the activities supported through Component 3 to a further 32 
countries.  
 
D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

 137. As described in the Project description section and the overall ToC, the BRACE project will lead to a paradigm shift 
and transformative change resulting in lasting impacts , building on the  interconnected pillars of the Comprehensive 
School Safety Framework (CSSF ). The outputs and activities of the project will contribute to significant results with 
regards to the safety of students . This will be  due to school infrastructural improvements and school safety and 
educational continuity management systems, and strengthened knowledge, skills , and capacities on climate 
change preparing students  to consider how climate  change will impact their lives and careers and what they can 
do to anticipate and adapt to projected climate impacts and still t hrive in lif e. The BRACE project also contributes 
to the strengthening of the enabling environment and mitigation of key barriers such as governance, gender and 
social inclusion, financial, technological, and institutional  barriers ; 
• Governance: resources and capacity for an improved enabling environment will  contribute to improved 
governance for climate change action in the education sector, e.g. through Component 3 coordination and 
collaboration will take place at scale.  
• Gender and social inclusion: Special attention will be paid to gender and social inclusion ensuring that youth, 
children, women, girls , and people with disabilities will be represented in decision- making, that interventions 
will pay specific attention to their needs and capacities and that , as a result of the project , their capabilities will 
be strengthened.  
• Financial: The project will partially close the existing financial gap in resources required to increase the climate 
resilience of the education sector. Additionally, it will build on and enhance initiatives, such as CSESI and 
country -specific initiatives e.g.  PCASS in Tonga, to bring more climate finance to least developed and climate 
vulnerable countries  (the CSESI project targets 35 of the world’s mo st climate vulnerable countries) . 
• Institutional: Support to school safety and educational continuity management systems will normalise and 
institutionalise climate resilience practices from central Ministry  of Education school management teams to 
school s. Additionally, the project will build institutional capacity through tool development, enhanced 
coordination (building on existing coordination system s such as the Education in Emergencies Clusters)  as 
well as target ed capacity development. T rainings  and refresher trainings will be recorded for institutional 
memory  so they can be reused beyond the life of the project.  
• Technological and informational: Through BRACE investments into school safety and educational continuity 
mechanisms , students will be prepared for a climate- resilient future and climate- related career pathways.  E.g. 
in Tonga , through offering Internet connectivity, schools will be able to access CIS and EWS as well as the 
Hama e- learning platforms , an innovative digital educational continuity mechanism. Additionally, through the 
development of a new climate change elective, students will be able to build their technical and practical skills 
and knowledge on climate change, preparing them for climate- related jobs.  
138. The BRACE project contributes to climate- resilient development pathways consistent with sustainable 
development, climate change, and educational strategies and plans. The project’s paradigm shift  will also be 
achieved through the delivery of 4 pr oject co -benefits  and will be underpinned by:  
• Targeted school -aged children and teachers with significantly increased knowledge of current and projected 
climate change impacts , increased skills for anticipating and adapting  to future climate change impact and 
foundations for climate- related professions/careers through climate change- related curriculum and climate 
elective s. 
• Targeted schools with safer and more climate- resilient facilities supported by enhanced access to climate 
information and early warnings  
• Targeted schools with strengthened climate and disaster preparedness and awareness , and school safety and 
resilience plans  to ensure educational continuity in time of climate- induced disasters.  
• Targeted Ministries of Education and Ministries  of Climate in particular, working in alignment to access climate 
finance, to refine NAPs and to embed climate- related educational policies, plans, programming and budgeting  
to support resilience strengthening . 
• Targeted engagement and communications  through coordination platform and Sout -South shared learnings . 
 
Scalability and replicability 
139. This project has been designed from the outset with both scalability and replicability  in mind , in align ment with, 
supporting the achievement of national polic y objectives . BRACE also has high complementarity and coherence 
with other relevant climate- focused projects. The CSSF model will  be scaled up to ensure additional schools will 
receive infrastructural improvements ( output 1.2), implementation of school safety and educational continuity 
management systems ( output 1.3), and development of  climate education curriculum  and teacher training  (output 
1.4) which will support and reach more students. Outcome 1 will also deliver specific  materials which can be used 
for scaling and replicating elsewhere  and ada pted to relevant country contexts  – this includes standards, guidelines 
and plans, ToT approaches, approaches in strengthening CIS and EWS, Climate -related School Safety Plans  and


---
*Page 44*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 38 
   
38 
 how to embed climate knowledge and action through formal and informal educational settings.  The climate finance 
tools and resources produced and disseminated through Components 2 and 3 will also ensure scalability  and 
replicability.  
Knowledge sharing and learning agenda  
140. Part of each C omponent will be dedicated to knowledge sharing and learning engagement between different 
stakeholders focused on school safety . This will be through annual lessons learned and reflection workshops , a 
learning agenda that will be implemented and shared internally in the three countries , and recordings on e- learning 
platforms that can share  knowledge  more broadly. L essons will be shared across all the regions t hrough PCASS 
and CSESI, leveraging existing regional information and coordination platforms . This will also ensure that BRACE 
has a high potential for knowledge sharing and learning and the CSSF will simultaneously be rolled out in three 
different countries.  
141. Through C omponents 2 and 3, BRACE will showcase how climate action can be scaled up in the education sector 
by successfully applying the CSSF model in different contexts with diverse climate risks and vulnerabilities and 
different educational systems. Components 2 and 3 have a strong focus on knowledge sharing and cross -country 
learning through capacity building to Ministries of Education on climate finance and engaging in other UNFCCC 
processes such as NAP development  in the three target countries. Knowledge products on these topics will be 
prepared and disseminated through a dedicated climate change and education knowledge management platform 
and through webinars and cross -country learning events  for the three target countries .  
142. The project will evaluate the impact of interventions, and studies are envisaged within the MEAL framework, to 
identify causal impact of the interventions. Both BRACE and CSESI will support learning events as part of the 
research studies, which will be attended by a range of government, NGO , and institutional stakeholders. Many of 
the interventions are relatively new within the country  context s and as such BRACE is in a unique position to test 
the ideas on a moderate scale and share key findings with all relevant bodies.   
 
D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)


---
*Page 45*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 39 
   
39 
 143. BRACE aligns with several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). Specifically: SDG4: Quality education for all; 
SDG5: Achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls; SDG 6: Ensure availability and sustainable 
management of water and sanitation for all; SDG11: Sustainable cities and communities, through making human 
settlements inclusive, safe, resilient and sustainable; SDG13: Take urgent action to combat climate change and 
its impacts.  
144. There are three  co-benefits delivered through the BRACE project:  
 
Co-benefit 1 : Gender and s ocial inclusion BRACE will indirectly benefit 4,128,574 females  during implementation. 
However, it is expected that  the focus on gender in BRACE should result in wider GESI benefits within the education 
sector, beyond what is delivered in the core project , based on social attitude and behavioural changes arising from the 
interventions.  This also includes the engagement of stakeholders during Components 2 and 3 and the learning and 
knowledge sharing opportunities through which the continued advocacy  of gendered climate  needs,  impacts and action 
will be raised. In alignment with the logframe ’s co -benefits  school  gendered, age appropriate and disability -friendly  
WaSH  facilities will be provided. These are also likely to be used by the community , either at times of extreme weather 
events or during community forums r. There will be further gender co -benefits, as the project will implement activities 
promoting menstrual hygiene, and creating dignity kits for girls and teachers at schools, allowing for knock -on capacity 
building at a wider community level. Improving the WASH facilities in close proximity to schools and communities could 
reduce the burden on women and girls who bear the brunt of activities related to water collection and washing. BRACE  
will deliver  gender empowerment  through community participation in activities, particularly forming community -wide 
DRR committees which involve children, women and people with disabilities  in remote communities . New or updated 
risk assessments and DRR plans will include needs and recommendations on GESI. Equally, GESI specific needs will 
be addressed in climate -related school safety plans. EWS information dissemination will adopt language which is age, 
gender and disability responsive.     
 
Co-benefit 2 : Environmental  impact  through school greening  via reduced emissions from l infrastructure paired with 
the use of PV panels for lighting and connectivity; in addition to Natural R esource Management (NRM) initiatives 
including tree planting and water efficient irrigation systems, for shading, windbreaks and cooling effects. Supporting 
school food gardens may encourage wider community uptake of positive environmental management practices . Also, 
through improved WASH facilities, including latrines, improved waste management systems  and improved hygiene 
practices . 
 Co-benefit 3 : Social:  Increased Locally- led Adaptation (LLA) - Influencing the LLA agenda working at the local 
school level with children, resident teachers and parents/community members. LLA can be amplified through project 
deliverables such as the building and implementation of school safety plans, child clubs, NAP participation, finance 
roadmap development and collaboration/south- south learning. Such interventions lend themselves to LLA such as 
through devolved decision- making and inclusion of marginalised people. School community -led initiatives and 
engagement may amplify further through knowledge sharing as well as opportunities for advocacy at key events such 
as climate dialogues e.g. COPs, Community Based Adaptation events. Ground -up design and impl ementation of 
climate action is strongly aligned with the principles of Locally -led Adaptation which will empower children as key 
stakeholders in decision- making and taking climate action. This co- benefit is achieved through Outcomes 1, 2 and 3.  
 
D.4. Needs of recipient  (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
 
The recipients  of BRACE include schools, school communities (children, parents, teachers and those living nearby to 
the school) and the education sector  as a whole.  The education sector's vulnerability to climate change is evident in 
the direct impact on school infrastructure, the indirect effects on learning and health, and the broader economic and 
social consequences. These vulnerabilities underscore the urgent need for climate- resilient investment and action in 
education systems to withstand and adapt to the continued and worsening challenges posed by a changing climate.  
The vulnerabilities are caused by increasing temperatures and erratic rainfall leading to hazards in Cambodia, South 
Sudan and Tonga of:  droughts, heatwaves, drought -related wildfires,  rising sea levels, coastal erosion, floods  and 
flashfloods  and storms/strong winds . Such hazards impact schools, their communities and the system as a whole , 
leading to increased vulnerability . Lack of financial investment in the education system to address these needs 
exacerbates  the vulnerability.


---
*Page 46*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 40 
   
40 
 In order to  foster a society that is informed and capable of responding to climate change challenges, the climate 
change vulnerabilities in the education sector must be addressed. The education sector must integrate climate 
resilience into planning and operations, which includes constructing climate- resilient infrastructure, developing 
disaster risk reduction strategies, incorporating climate change into the curriculum, build pedagogical capacity and 
content knowledge among teachers and educators, and supporting commu nities to adapt to climate change 
impacts.   
 
Cambodia  
145. Cambodia is one of the poorest countries among the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) 
characterised by limited educational opportunities, high income inequality and poor job prospects .89 The country’s 
vulnerability to climate change is linked to its characteristics as a post ‐civil war, least developed, predominantly 
agrarian country, with 76% of the population living in rural areas.90 Along with the Philippines, Cambodia is 
regarded by the United Nations and the International Development Research Center as Southeast Asia's most 
vulnerable nation to the consequences of climate change.91 The ND-GAIN  ranks Cambodia  149th most vulnerable 
country out of 187, with equally low rankings for vulnerability (130) and climate readiness (158)92. Cambodia faces 
severe climate change vulnerabilities, ranking as the second most vulnerable country globally to climate hazards 
according to the 2024 INFORM risk index.93 
 
146. Additionally, Cambodia ranks 46th out of 163 countries94 in UNICEF’s Children’s Climate Risk Index based on 
climate and environmental shocks, which is in the top third of countries with a high risk to climate change The 
UNICEF media release found that Cambodian children are highly exposed to water scarcity, riverine flooding and 
vector -borne disease. Furthermore, the Global Climate Risk Index ranks Cambodia as 14th most climate risk prone 
country globally95. The country is particularly vulnerable to the hazards of floods, droughts, heatwaves, cyclones , 
and seawater intrusion due to its geographic location.  During these climate events , school infrastructure is 
damaged, children are increasingly absent losing learning and development opportunities  and school comm unities 
suffer climate related health illnesses.  
 
South Sudan  
147. As an LDC, South Sudan is recognised as being particularly vulnerable to economic shocks while having urgent 
and immediate adaptation needs. South Sudan is ranked by the ND -GAIN as the 8th most vulnerable country in 
the world to climate shocks, and the 175th most ready country to adapt to the impacts of climate change. South 
Sudan is also close to the bottom of all countries in terms of Human Development, ranking 185th out of 189 
countries on the Human Development Index and an estimated 80% of the population live beneath the absolute 
poverty line.96 Conflict is also still extremely prevalent in South Sudan, and is a core driver of humanitarian needs.   
 
148. This project focuses on adaptation needs for recipients  in six counties extreme flooding, drought, temperature 
related wildfires and heatwaves which render learners unable to go to school. South Sudan’s topographic context 
– extreme lowlands and swamps – mean that schools and communities are extremely vulnerabl e to flooding, which 
is growing increasingly worse due to erratic and intense rainfall, leading hundreds of schools across the country to 
close and become damaged beyond repair.  
 
Tonga  
149. Tonga’s school children, surrounding communities and the educational system is already - and will continue to - 
face sea level rise, droughts, floodings, and extreme weather including tropical cyclones and heatwaves. Climate 
change generates intense challenges to Tonga’s education system. Tonga does  not have any climate change 
specific courses in schools and during stakeholder consultations, government stakeholders emphasized the need 
to guide and prepare students for climate professions.  
150. A World Bank report97, confirmed by consultations with government stakeholders, indicated that schools especially 
on the outer islands are highly vulnerable to climate risks and urgently need infrastructural support. Past climate

 events in 2018 and 2020, were highly disruptive of children’s education and associated with high financial costs .

151. Unfortunately, domestic and international funding sources are insufficient to meet the adaptation needs of the 
education sector and the dispersed school system. Most international donor -supported programmes focus on 
immediate development priorities with only a minor focus on climate change, and often with a mitigation focus .  
152. Components 2 and 3 focus on mobilising climate finance  and building knowledge and networks which are greatly 
lacking in the target countries. It’s clear that as climate impacts and risks increase , the viability of any investments 
in the education sector will be compromised.  Stakeholders need to be engaged and coordinated in order to 
collaborate, access finance, prioritise climate actions and share knowledge and lessons  with a view to strengthen 
the country’s cl imate resilience in the education sector.  
D.5. Country ownership  (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)


---
*Page 48*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 42 
   
42 
 153. BRACE is designed to enhance the resilience of the education sectors in South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga to 
the impacts of climate change. Country ownership is embedded through BRACE’s strong partnerships with 
government ministries and extensive stakeholder engagement, in addition to alignment with the national policies, 
climate change strategies, and gender action plans of these countries. BRACE aims to support national climate 
and education priorities while addressing common challenges faced by these diverse regions.  
 
Policy Alignment and Support 
154. BRACE aligns with the national climate change strategies and educational policies of South Sudan, Cambodia, 
and Tonga ensuring that the project supports each country’s efforts to build climate- resilient education systems. In 
South Sudan, the project aligns with the NAP and the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs ), prioritizing 
climate adaptation in education. Similarly, in Cambodia , BRACE supports the Cambodia Climate Change Strategic 
Plan (CCCSP) 2014 - 2023 and the National Action Plan for Disaster Risk Reduction (NAP -DRR), which emphasize 
integrating climate resilience into the education sector. Tonga’s  alignment is reflected through its Strategic 
Development Framework (TSDF) 2015- 2025 and the Joint National Action Plan for Climate Change Adaptation 
and Disaster Risk Management (JNAP II) 2018- 2028, which prioritize building resilience across all sectors,  
including education.  
155. In each country, BRACE will support the implementation of national strategies by improving the climate resilience 
of school infrastructure, integrating climate change education into curricula, and enhancing disaster preparedness. 
By aligning with these policies, BRACE contributes directly to the broader climate adaptation goals of South Sudan, 
Cambodia, and Tonga ensuring that educational continuity is maintained despite increasing climate risks.  
 
Alignment with Gender Action Plans and Policies  
156. Please see Annex 8  for more information.  The project is designed to be gender -responsive and more broadly 
inclusive to address the unique needs of girls, children with disabilities, women, and internally displaced people. In 
South Sudan, the project aligns with the National Gender Policy, ensuring that gender perspectives are integrated 
into all aspects of climate adaptation and disaster risk reduction. Cambodia’s  Gender Mainstreaming Strategic 
Plan in Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management is supported by BRACE’s efforts to ensure that both girls 
and boys have equal access to climate- resilient educational facilities. Similarly, in Tonga , BRACE aligns with the 
national education policy framework, ensuring that interventions are inclusive of all genders and children with 
disabilities.  
 
Complementarity and Coherence with Government Policies and Plans  
157. BRACE is designed to complement existing government initiatives and frameworks, ensuring coherence with 
national development plans and sectoral strategies in South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga. The project leverages 
ongoing efforts such as the Climate Smart Education Systems Initiative (CSESI) in South Sudan , which supports 
climate -resilient infrastructure and educational continuity. In Cambodia , BRACE complements the National 
Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan (NBSAP) and the Rectangular Strategy Phase IV, which prioritize 
environmental sustainability and natural resource management. Tonga  benefits from the project’s alignment with 
previous initiatives like the Outer Island Renewable Energy Project (OIREP) and the GCF FP090 Tonga 
Renewable Energy Project (TREP), which focus on enhancing the resilience of school infrastructure and improving 
access to renewable energy and internet connectivity.  
158. By building on these initiatives, BRACE maximizes its impact and contributes to a coordinated national response 
to climate change in each country. The project’s design ensures that interventions are integrated into existing 
frameworks, allowing for the efficient use of resources and ensuring that project activities are sustainable and 
scalable.  
 
Key Partner Engagement and Government Leadership  
159. The successful implementation of BRACE depends on strong partnerships with government ministries and 
leadership from local authorities in each country. In South Sudan , the Ministry of General Education and 
Instruction (MoGE&I), the Ministry of Environment, and the Ministry of Humanitarian Affairs and Disaster 
Management provide strategic oversight, ensuring that project activities align with national priorities. Similarly, in 
Cambodia , the Ministry of Education, Youth, and Sport (MoEYS) leads the project’s implementation, working 
closely with local governments and development partners to integrate climate resilience into the education sector. 
In Tonga , the Ministry of Education and Training (MET) and the Ministry of Meteorology, Energy, Information, 
Disaster Management, Environment, Climate Change and Communications (MEIDECC) provide leadership, 
ensuring that BRACE aligns with national climate adaptation goals.  
160. In each country, BRACE engages local governments, community organizations, and school management 
committees ensuring that interventions are contextually relevant and sustainable. By fostering a sense of 
ownership among local stakeholders the project ensures that the benefits of climate resilience are maintained long 
after the project’s completion.

 Ownership and Stakeholder Consultations  
161. Please see Annex 7 for more information. Stakeholder engagement has been a critical component of BRACE ’s 
development and implementation in all three countries. Extensive consultations with government agencies, local 
communities, non- governmental organizations, and development partners have ensured that the project aligns 
with local needs and priorities. In South Sudan, consultations informed the project’s focus on strengthening the 
resilience of school infrastructure and integrating climate change education into the curriculum. In Cambodia , 
discussions with national and sub- national stakeholders helped identify key areas of intervention, ensuring that the 
project is responsive to local capacities and vulnerabilities. Similarly, in Tonga, participatory processes involving 
government agencies and local communities ensured that the project is well -aligned with local challenges and 
opportunities related to climate change adaptation.  
162. These consultations have not only shaped the project’s design but also fostered broad support for its 
implementation. BRACE will continue to engage stakeholders throughout the project implementation, ensuring 
ongoing alignment with national and community priorities , and enabling adaptive management based on 
stakeholder feedback.  
163. The involvement of local communities and educational institutions in the project’s design and implementation will 
further ensure that interventions are contextually appropriate and have a lasting impact.  
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness ( max`. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
The BRACE Project’s expected impact.  With an estimated total budget of USD 4 6.7 million, the project's GCF grant 
is estimated at USD 40.7 million, with a grant -co-financing ratio of 0.85 to 1. The project is expected to deliver to 8.6 
million  overall beneficiaries out of which over 60 8,000 will be directly influenced and 8. 64 million  will see indirect 
benefits.   
Concessionality justification.  The proposed concessional financing level for the BRACE project is justifiable based 
on the ongoing need for highly concessional financing in these three target  countries. This type of financing is vital for 
implementing interventions that will not only enhance the educational environment for students in public schools in 
three target  countries but also bring about a positive societal change. These countries are particularly vulnerable to the 
impacts of climate change and require increased public investment to adapt their education systems accordingly. Since 
basic education shows qualities of public good98, it is typically funded by governments using national taxes or other 
grant funding, as the public good attributes make it difficult or costly for private firms to appropriate rents99. As a result, 
the displacement of the private sector is not expected to be a concern in these target  countries once the BRACE project 
is underway.  
Budgetary constraints in target  countries.  Various factors, including budgetary, macroeconomic, and internal 
government organization aspects, contribute to this need for grant financing:  
1. Cambodia:  The country's fiscal deficit widened in 2023 due to extended support to households and firms 
affected by the COVID -19 pandemic. This unprecedented crisis and increased spending associated with the 
elections and the 2023 South- East Asia Games have significantly strained the country's resources. The public 
debt ratio to GDP is expected to increase moderately in the next ten years, further limiting funding options for 
public goods, including climate change adaptation of the education system.100 
2. South Sudan:  South Sudan continues to experience severe humanitarian and economic challenges resulting 
from several shocks, including the Red Sea crisis, the war in Sudan, domestic policy deficiencies, and climate 
change calamities (e.g., flooding). The country has si gnificant financing needs, including social and 
development spending needs, debt service obligations on a large stock of non- concessional external debt, 
reserve coverage of below one month of imports against a background of a projected decline in oil prices, and 
a continued downward global trend for international aid.  The public resources for education and climate 
adaptation remain scarce. 101 
3. Tonga:  Despite the challenges, Tonga's economy has been recovering well from the COVID -19 pandemic. The 
tourism sector, a key contributor to Tonga's economy, is in the process of rebuilding after the pandemic and 
climate -related shocks. However, the need for infrastructure rebuilding and labour shortages may slow down 
this process. Tonga's long -term growth is projected at 1.2 percent, given its exposure to natural disasters and 
 
98 The public good concept encompasses non- rivalrous and non- excludable goods. While some argue that private basic education or higher 
education exists, it may not fit this definition due to the existence of private schools or universities. Basic schooling is generally considered a 
public good. Education benefits every member of society, making it essential for all of us to contribute to its funding through taxes. This investment in education is crucial as it leads to a more educated population, ultimately benefiting society.

Consequently, taking into consideration (i) the significant climate- related vulnerabilities of these target  countries that

public good type of interventions, and (iii) the fact that target  countries suffer from serious budgetary pressures, the 
proposed grant financing for the BRAC E project is justified.  
EFA modelling results. The ex -ante EFA was pursued separately for each of the three target  countries in economic 
terms from two perspectives: (1) the perspective of direct beneficiaries (in individual/per average and indicative public 
primary school type and in aggregate per assumed number of similar schools to obtain BRACE interventions in each  
of the three countries, and (2) the perspective of the entire BRACE project with an embedded accounting of the 
budgetary implementation framework of 5 (4 in the case of South Sudan) years. These two modelling perspectives (1) 
and (2) were additionally appraised using two different climate scenario assumptions, RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5, to show 
how the results might change under differing climate hardships. The EFA results  were calculated over 15 years using 
a conservative discount rate of 9%. The aggregate Economic estimates from the entire project perspective to be 
targeted by the BRACE project are listed in the Table below. The detailed per -target -country results can be seen in

Application of the best practices.  The BRACE project is dedicated to maximizing impact by implementing proven 
interventions and best practices in targeted schools. Using a participatory and integrated approach, our objective is to

future- oriented, and data-

education policies and strategies that are 
tailored to each countries 
national context .
 
Replicability  Government structures in South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga should enable 
unified planning, roll out, 
and implementation of climate resilient, and 
gender equitable policies 
and strategies. However, 
institutional capacity 
constraints at local, sub-national , and national 
levels hamper effective adaptation planning 
processes in the 
education sector, inter - 
and intra- ministerial 
coordination, access to climate and education 
finance, and implementation of 
adaptation actions at 
scale. These constraints also mean th at 
governments have  
limited focus on internal 
knowledge sharing and 
dissemination across Low  Ministries of education specialise 
in the replication of systems and processes, and train teachers to 
educate children though Kindergarten to 12 (or similar) 
cycles. They apply standardised 
methods and tools (e.g., 
curriculum and testing) and are 
expert in the replication of their usage.  
The BRACE project will provide 
ministries of education with the 
knowledge, capacities, tools, and 
systems to integrate climate 
resilience (through improved 
infrastructure, management and 
curriculum) into their existing systems, which are designed for 
replication. The project will 
support the development, testing, assessment, and adaptation if 
needed in preparation for rollout at scale. The BRACE design is 
focused on supporting the 
education system so activities can 
be replicated and scale up, as The replication of output 1.1 is intended to spread 
beyond the 3 countries of implementation. Policies, plans, and systems established in the 3 
countries do not require national replication but rather periodic updating. However, Components 2 
& 3 will seek to replicate good practice through 
sharing lessons learned in the 3 countries, and 
more broadly through interested institutions (UN 
agencies, development banks, donors, and NGOs) engaged in Component 3.     
 
The activities and outcomes of output 1.2 
(infrastructure),  output 1.3 ( continuity 
management ), and output 1.4 (curriculum) will be 
designed for replication nationally in geographies not reached by the project intervention. In some 
circumstances resources will be required (such as 
infrastructure) and if not available, this could hinder the replication of activities. However 
management practices and curriculum can be 
replicated at a relatively low cost by embedding them in existing systems that benefit the country 
as a whole (such as teacher training or the development of management capacity).


---
*Page 54*
---

E 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 48 
  
  
48 
 sectors and limited 
participation efforts in 
global learning focused 
on climate change and 
education.   designed for all other educational 
activities.  
Sustainability  The introduction of new 
strategies and solutions 
in South Sudan, 
Cambodia,  and Tonga 
often fail due to the 
following: a lack of official 
government 
endorsement ; insufficient 
investment in training 
and capacity  
strengthening;  weak 
systems and procedures 
to maintain infrastructure;  
and a lack of widespread community participation, 
particularly from groups 
in rural  and remote 
areas , women, girls , and 
boys , PWDs and other 
groups that experience 
inequality and 
discrimination.  
 Low  The target scenario is to 
effectively embed into the education system an understating 
of the impacts of climate change, 
and to provide the tools, systems, 
policies, plans, capacities, and 
capabilities for respective 
ministries of education to make 
necessary strengthening and 
adaptions to their existing ways of 
working. This will build the climate resilience of the education 
system, schools, and the children 
they teach. As noted above 
ministries of education are set up 
to replicate and scale up systems 
and process sustainably. 
Therefore, the project will focus 
on embedding the work deep into 
education systems and processes 
where knowledge and 
understanding will be sustained.  
 
   Output 1.1 focuses on the enabling systems that 
will allow for the sustainability of actions. If climate 
resilient systems and policies are integrated into 
standard ways of working in ministries of 
education they are likely to be sustained long 
term. Work to date through the CSESI parallel 
project shows overwhelming relevance and 
timeliness to ministries of education, which  will support the sustainability of ongoing work in these 
3 countries.     
  
 
Work will be sustained, w hen designs  and 
standards  for school facility construction , 
operation,  and maintenance (as part of Output 
1.2) are updated, used, and monitored, and 
include considerations of climate change impacts.  
 
When activities of Output 1.4 are complete, and 
curriculum and teacher training material 
developed, disseminated and tested, teacher 
training and its practical application and impacts 
will be sustainable.  
 
The Component 3 design consultation highlighted 
that there is no obvious organisation, body, or 
existing coordination mechanism that is currently


---
*Page 55*
---

E 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 49 
  
  
49 
 suited to sustain coordination of efforts on 
integrating climate change and education. 
Therefore Component 3 will focus on enriching 
dialogue and collaboration and exploring the suitability of existing mechanisms to take on global 
coordination in the long term. The Steering 
Committee of Component 3 will be drawn from the key organisation invested in this area of work, and 
will be tasked with identifying the most suitable 
home for climate and education to be sustained in 
the long term.

5. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 300 words)  
Besides the arrangements (e.g. annual performance reports) laid out in Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA), please give a summary of the project/programme 
specific arrangements for monitoring, reporting and evaluation including a description of the monitoring and reporting system that will be used to assess the climate 
results of the proposed project/programme. Please also summarize the types of interim and final evaluations. Describe Accredited Entity (AE) project reporting 
relationships, including to the National Designated Authority (NDA)/Focal Point and between AE and Executing Entity (EE) as relevant, identifying reporting o bligations 
from the EE to the AE.  
  
164. A full M&E plan for BRACE will include detailed information on the roles and responsibilities for data collection and management, information 
flows and reporting systems, finalized indicators and means of verifications, monitoring protocols and tools, implementation plans and schedules will 
be defined within 6 months of project inception. Output data will be collected an ongoing basis as part of monitoring activities to track progress against 
each output indicator to understand whether the project is hitting key milestones, to be analysed regularly to allow for learning and adaptation and 
reported annually. Outcome indicators will be reported midline and endline aligning with the cadence of evaluations. Baseline and midline impact 
evaluations will be conduct ed by SCI, and at endline impact evaluation will be conducted by an external party, under SCI supervision. An independent


---
*Page 107*
---

 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 101 
  
  
101 
 process midterm evaluation will be conducted to assess project progress against the outcome indicators and recommend improvem ents for the last 
two years of the project.  
  
165. The monitoring and evaluation activities will questions related to enabling environment and policy change with qualitative methods and employ 
quantitative approaches including child and school staff surveys from a sample of target schools participating in the project to measure changes in 
knowledge and attitudes related to climate change and disaster risk management and whether the project mitigated learning los s. A digitized data 
collection system will be established in this sample of target schools to track student attendance, absenteeism and dropout based on reporting from 
school administrators and confirmed by MEAL staff on a quarterly basis.

 RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT  
F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)   
 
Selected Risk Factor 1 – Low Technical Capacity  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and Operational  High Medium  
Description 
167. Limited technical capacity among local stakeholders, including education authorities and community members, 
could hinder the effective implementation and sustainability of climate adaptation measures in South Sudan, 
Cambodia, and Tonga, potentially leading to suboptimal project outcomes.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
168. The BRACE initiative will implement a comprehensive capacity -building program targeting government officials, 
local communities, and project partners across South Sudan, Cambodia, and Tonga. This program will focus on 
enhancing technical knowledge in climate -smart education systems, disaster risk reduction, and child protection in 
schools, aligned with national frameworks like Cambodia's Comprehensive School Safety Framework (CSSF) and the Child -Friendly School Program. To support this, BRACE will establish  national and sub- national platforms for 
knowledge sharing and coordination, particularly through activities such as virtual and offline child- and-youth- led 
campaigns for climate action and cross -coordination meetings between ministries and relevant actors. Regular 
monitoring and mentoring mechanisms will be put in place to ensure the application of learned skills and 
knowledge, with continuous technical support provided to local stakeholders to foster long- term capacity 
development. Additionally, partnersh ips with local universities and technical institutes will be sought out and 
established to strengthen the sustainability of these capacity -building efforts. Operationally lessons learned from 
the AE’s existing GCF portfolio are being incorporated into the systems and process used to oversight and moni tor 
overall project progress . This includes  an emphasis  on operation frameworks , planning, clarity on accountabilities 
and regular coordination between AE and EEs.   
Selected Risk Factor 2 – Climate Vulnerability  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Other  Medium  Medium  
Description 
169. High vulnerability to climate change impacts, such as extreme weather events and sea -level rise, could disrupt 
project activities and threaten the safety and livelihoods of target communities in South Sudan, Cambodia, and 
Tonga.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
170. BRACE will incorporate climate risk assessments into its planning and implementation phases to ensure that 
interventions are resilient to local climate conditions. In Tonga, for instance, the project will prioritize retrofitting school infrastructure to withstand extreme weather, including improving airflow, drainage, and accessibility. In Cambodia, BRACE will support the establishment of school -
based early warning systems linked to national 
platforms for real -time impact monitoring, enabling schools to better prepare for and respond to climate- related 
events. The project will also promote adaptive agricultural practices and live lihood diversification to enhance 
community resilience. Infrastructure improvements, such as climate- resilient WASH facilities, will be prioritized to 
protect vulnerable populations. Emergency response plans will be developed and regularly updated, with ongoing collaboration with meteorological agencies and climate experts to provide data and insights that inform adaptive management. Regular drills and simulations will be conducted to ensure readiness for climate- related disruptions, 
and partnerships with l ocal communities will be fostered to strengthen local capacities for disaster risk reduction.  
Selected Risk Factor 3 – Fraud and Corruption 
Category  Probability  Impact  
ML/FT  Medium  Low


---
*Page 109*
---

E 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 103 
   
103 
 Description 
171. The risk of fraud and corruption in financial management and procurement processes could lead to the 
misallocation of funds, compromising the project's integrity and outcomes.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
172. Key mitigation measures will include strict adherence to anti -fraud and anti -corruption policies, compliance with 
national institutional structures, and the application of robust monitoring and reporting procedures. BRACE will 
enforce anti -fraud and anti -corruption policies through rigorous audits and transparent reporting mechanisms, with 
oversight provided by an independent PIU teams. Digital tools will be employed to track expenditures and 
procurement processes, reducing opportunities for corruption. Fur thermore, all project staff and partners will 
undergo mandatory training on ethical standards, anti -corruption practices, and the consequences of non-
compliance. A whistleblower protection mechanism will be established to encourage the reporting of suspici ous 
activities without fear of retaliation. Regular risk assessments will be conducted to identify and address any 
emerging vulnerabilities in financial management, and specific procedures, such as the separation of duties 
between project management and ex ecuting entities, will be employed to maintain independent and effective 
financial oversight.  
Selected Risk Factor 4 – Construction Risk  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and Operational  Low Medium  
Description  
173. Lack of design input and requisite supervisory budget results in poor quality of design and poor 
workmanship.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
174. Supervision will be carried out by qualified Save the Children Structural Engineers/Construction Managers or a 
qualified third party. Technical consultations will include the Save the Children Regional Office Construction 
Advisor to ensure safe programming and appropriate project design. At least 10% of the budget is dedicated to 
supervision and also covers construction costs, staff supervision, and fleet requirements. Safety and security 
assessments will be conducted with guidance from the country office safety focal point and GEDSI specialist. The retrofitting design aims to enhance building resilience and will include planning that covers site surveys, water and 
power supply assessments, site access conditions, hazard risk assessments, environmental impac t 
considerations, building consent acquisition, and market surveys for material availability. Adequate time will be 
allocated for the recruitment and induction of technical staff, as well as for design/assessment, site selection 
(including Safety Audit and Accessibility Assessment), tendering, contractor assessment, construction 
implementation, approval processes, and accounting for potential seasonal interruptions due to weather. The 
proposed rehabilitation/retrofitting of school buildings will undergo str uctural engineering assessment by a qualified 
civil engineer and will incorporate design principles for vulnerable beneficiaries, integrated WASH, and inclusion 
measures.
