 GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS  
G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment  (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)   
175. The proposed project activities have been screened against the Save the Children Project Program 
Environmental and Social Sustainability Management System (PESSMS) which is aligned with the GCF 
Environmental and Social Policy (ESP). Below is a summary of the risks identified during PPF screening, 
confirming the risk categorisation of C . The programme is likely to have minimal adverse environmental or 
social impacts, as  they have been avoided through design or can easily be minimised through implementation 
of standard management measures.  
176. An Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP) was developed during PPF to provide detailed 
guidance during implementation. The ESAP is provided in Annex 6 of this Funding Proposal. The main risks anticipated are summarised against the GCF ESMF standard be low along with mitigation measures to address 
them.  
  
Summary of Risks  Mitigation Factors/Measures or Management Strategies  
ESS 2 Labour and working conditions  
 
The project proposes  activities including the 
retrofitting of schools to make them more resilient  to 
the projected impacts of climate change.  Project activities will include some small- scale physical 
works (e.g., retrofitting of WASH facilities or infrastructure 
to support PV arrays) that may potentially result in 
exposure to typical physical health and safety risks 
associated with small scale construction for workers. This risk is assessed as low since all these activities are small -
scale and potential for injury would be minimal.  
 
There is the potential for small- scale physical works 
requiring labourers to employ children in some project 
countries. This risk is assessed as low since the activities 
will be taking place within known and controllable areas 
(i.e. schools) where mitigation measures can be readily 
implemented and effectively monitored.    
ESS 3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution 
Prevention  
Small quantities of concrete will be required for 
concrete footings/foundations of some intervention types such as WASH infrastructure or solar panels 
potentially leading to localised contamination or soils 
from concrete slurry or wastewater.  
With concrete use, there will be small quantities of concrete slurry waste which can be caustic to skin.  
  
  The ESAP provides management measures for the use of 
concrete.  
Concrete will be prepared on bunded and covered hard 
stand surface.  
All wastewater from concrete production will be collected to 
allow particulates to settle out before being discharged.  
Slurry from concrete production will be collected allowed to harden. Solid and cured concrete waste is considered safe 
to be reused by the community for infrastructure 
maintenance.  
Workers will be provided with appropriate PPE.  
This is considered low risk  given the expected small 
quantities of concrete required and as the management 
measures are known to be effective and easily 
implemented.  
ESS 6: Biodiversity Conservation and 
Sustainable Management of Living Resources   
The project proposed to develop gardens in South 
Sudan schools which has the potential to introduce 
invasive plant species.  Drought resilient seeds will be planted at schools in South 
Sudan.  Under the project’s activity exclusion list, only 
species which do not have the potential to become 
invasive will be permitted.


---
*Page 111*
---

F 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 105 
   
105 
 This is considered low risk as the project has put measures 
in place to avoid the use of invasive species and the gardens 
are being planted in are within already altered environments.  
ESS 7: Indigenous Peoples  
While Tonga, South Sudan and Cambodia have 
populations which are considered indigenous or 
minority ethnic, at the project site intervention level 
across the program, school communities are 
considered to be homogenous in language, culture 
and practices. Therefore, Project benefits or impacts will not adversely affect indigenous people under the 
GCF definition at the Project site level.  
All physical activities will take place within existing 
defined school footprints where the school has 
documented a lease or secure usage rights of that 
land.  
Sub activities linked to policies and strategies 
proposed by the project relate to education policy and 
school infrastructure resilience. No lands or resources 
used by indigenous people other minority groups will 
be subject to any policies or plans developed by the 
project.  
There are no project activities which would require Free, Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC)  An Indigenous Peoples Plan (IPP) is not required for this 
project as there are no activities impacting the lands or 
resources of indigenous groups and there are no activities 
for which FPIC is required.  
To ensure best practice in consultation activities is used, 
traditional customary processes for each project country are 
integrated throughout Project design and stakeholder 
engagement.  
The project activity design as well as the SEP integrates the 
inclusion of any identified indigenous, minority, marginalised 
or vulnerable groups for sub activities: 1.1.1.2.b, 1.1.1.9.b, 
1.1.2.5.a, 1.1.2.5.b, 1.1.4.2.a, 1.1.4.1.b, 1.1.4.4.a and 
1.1.4.2.b.  
This is considered low risk  as no lands or resources used 
by indigenous people other minority groups are required for 
or impacted by this project.  
Gender Equity and Women’s Empowerment.  
 
The project included activities to support M inistries 
update and improve their National  Adaptation Plans 
and at a subnational level to develop school -base 
climate  resilience and school safety plans.  There is a risk that if engagement of women is not 
mainstreamed across the implementation of all project 
activities (particularly: the development of national plans, 
guidance or strategies related to climate resilience/DDR and 
education; the formation of w orking/stakeholder groups; 
capacity building opportunities), the Project might not fully 
incorporate or reflect views of women and girls or ensure equitable opportunities for their involvement and benefit.
  
 
This risk is considered low  as measures to avoid this risk 
are integrated into project design and M&E frameworks 
through the BRACE Gender Action Plan (GAP).   
 
Accountability and Grievance Mechanism  
There is a possibility that implementing partners are not adequately aware of their responsibilities under the ESS requirements in implementing the BRACE 
Project.  The PIU will be trained in the effective implementation of 
the Environmental and Social Action Plan and 
responsibilities within that plan will be clearly defined.  
  
This is considered of low risk or concern given the limited potential negative impacts associated with the BRACE 
interventions.

G.2.  Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)


---
*Page 113*
---

F 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 107 
   
107 
 178. The Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan was developed by gender experts across the three countries 
using primary and secondary data including key informant interviews and focus group discussions. The analysis 
presented in Annex 8 considers the ways in which gender and social norms intersect with adaptive capacities and 
climate change impacts across Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga. The assessment sets out key data and 
analysis across five domains and includes recommendations for the project across all c omponents and outputs. 
The Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Action Plan highlights GESI actions included in the project wide logical 
framework for the 3  countries as well as Components 2 and 3. The five domains of analysis are:  
• Laws, policies and institutions  
• Social norms, roles, responsibilities and t ime use 
• Participation, leadership and d ecision -making  
• Access to and c ontrol over resources and s ervices  
• Safety, dignity and w ell-being 
179. The project aims to improve the adaptive capacities and climate resilience of children, their communities and 
schools, recognising that children and their communities are impacted by climate change differently depending on many intersecting factors including, but not limited to, their age, gender, disability, ethnicity , race, economic status, 
diverse SOGIESC and refugee status.  
180. All three countries identified that women, girls, men, and boys are all impacted differently by the socially assigned 
discriminatory gender roles and responsibilities at the household and community levels because of the traditional social roles and power hierarchies within the family that are deeply ingrained in society. These affect the way 
marginalized groups are affected by climate change from access to education and climate information (including 
early warnings), increased gender -
based violence and child, early and forced marriage and unions, limited 
involvement in political and familial decision- making, and reduced control over land and resources.  
181. Women and girls are more vulnerable to climate- induced disasters due to economic and social inequalities. In 
Cambodia, women responsible for managing household resources face challenges accessing food, water, and energy during floods and droughts, while in  Tonga, remote communities are particularly vulnerable. The project 
will implement gender -
responsive adaptation strategies, including protecting women, girls, and people with 
disabilities. School safety plans and climate resilience measures will be develop ed to address their specific 
vulnerabilities, ensuring equitable access to support and resources.  
182. In South Sudan, patriarchal norms and conflict limit women’s participation in public life, particularly in climate and 
education decisions with displacement due to floods and conflict heightening the risk of violence against women 
and girls. To mitigate this, the project aims for gender -balanced representation in governance structures, aiming 
for 50% female participation.  
183. In Tonga, extreme weather events, such as cyclones, expose women and girls to GBV in evacuation centers. To 
mitigate this, the project integrates GBV prevention measures, including safe spaces, psychosocial support, and 
community awareness campaigns.  
184. Climate- related disruptions also jeopardize girls’ education. In Cambodia and South Sudan, traditional gender roles 
often force girls to prioritize household duties over schooling, particularly during and after climate extremes. The BRACE project will retr ofit school infrastructure in Cambodia, ensuring access to gender -
responsive water, 
sanitation, and hygiene (WASH) facilities. In South Sudan, where schools are frequently closed due to floods and 
conflict, safety plans will help ensure girls’ and other marginalized groups’ continued education.  
185. Additionally, analysis of historical data by Save the Children shows that a 10% increase or decrease in rainfall is 
associated with a 1% increase in CEFMU, globally. Globally almost 9 million girls face extreme risk of climate 
disasters and CEFMU every year and around two- thirds of CEFMU happen in regions with higher -than- average 
climate risks. Gender inequality is the root cause of CEFMU, but other risk factors increase the likelihood of a girl 
being married. These include being out -of-school, living in poverty, food shortages, being pregnant or having a 
baby and exposure to other forms of GBV. Climate disasters and the long- term impacts of the climate crisis can 
increase these risk factors. For example, disasters can lead to school closures and loss of inc ome for families. 
GBV also increases during crises and families may decide to remove girls from school or have them marry to keep 
them safe from violence or to make sure their daughters are provided for by another family.  
186. Finally, the exclusion of marginalized groups —including people with disabilities, ethnic minorities, and LGBTQI+ 
individuals —poses a significant risk. In Tonga, the leiti community faces discrimination, while people with 
disabilities in Cambodia and indigenous groups struggle to access education and climate services. To address 
this, the project ensures all activities are inclusive, providing educational materials in accessible formats such as braille, sign language, and multiple languages, enabling meaningful participation for all.  
187. For Components 2 and 3, the BRACE project will include GESI in NAP reviews, support child/youth- led and 
women’s organization representatives to engage in the BRACE Coordination Mechanism and ensure this is 
gender -balanced and include GESI in the developmen t and implementation of the Climate Change and Education 
Declaration Roadmap.


---
*Page 114*
---

F 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 108 
   
108 
 188. The BRACE project’s gender -responsive approach to climate resilience, tailored to the specific needs of Cambodia, 
South Sudan, and Tonga, aligns with the GCF’s Gender Policy.  
189. For more detailed analysis and mitigation strategies related to gender and social inclusion risks, please refer to 
Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Action Plan. The project will be monitored through gender -responsive 
indicators and have budget allocated to GESI activities to ensure equitable outcomes for all stakeholders.  
 
G.3.  Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)


---
*Page 115*
---

F 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 109 
   
109 
 190. Save the Children Australia (SCA), as the Accredited Entity (AE), will oversee financial management and 
procurement for the BRACE project, ensuring full compliance with Green Climate Fund (GCF) fiduciary standards. 
The project will be implemented in Tonga, Cambodia, and South Sudan through a decentralized structure, with 
each country having its own Project Implementation Unit (PIU) and Project Steering Committee (PSC). These 
entities will be responsible for day -to-day financial operations and procurement activities, while SCA provides 
overall strategic and fiduciary oversight.  
191. Financial Management Arrangements . The financial management structure for the BRACE project is informed 
by comprehensive financial management capacity assessments and due diligence conducted on all Executing 
Entities (EEs). These assessments, detailed in Annex 20: Know Your Customer AML/CFT Due Diligence 
Assessment , reviewed financial systems, procedures, and capacity in each country, resulting in tailored financial 
arrangements to match their specific needs.  
• Tonga : Save the Children Tonga (SCTON) is a newly registered legal entity, and its financial and procurement 
capacity is still in development. SCA’s assessment confirmed that SCTON’s systems will be aligned with those 
of Save the Children Australia. During the project’s start -up phase, SCA will provide training and capacity 
building on GCF -specific rules, policies, and reporting requirements to enhance SCTON’s capacity and reduce 
risks.  
• Cambodia : Save the Children International Cambodia (SCIKHM) was assessed as having sufficient financial 
management capacity to handle the project’s funds. With a strong track record of managing large- scale projects 
with international donors, SCIKHM has procurement  and financial systems well -aligned with GCF standards, 
ensuring smooth fund management and reporting processes.  
• South Sudan : Save the Children International South Sudan (SCISSD) operates in a more challenging 
environment. While SCISSD has a well -established financial management system, the due diligence 
assessment identified potential risks due to political instability and limited capacity within local government 
entities. To mitigate these risks, financial management will include phased disbursements, strict reporting 
requirements, and enhanced monitoring by SCA to ensure compliance.  
192. In all three countries,  funds will be distributed to EEs and implementing partners, based on work plans and budgets 
approved by the PSCs. The PIUs will collaborate closely with national ministries and implementing partners to 
ensure efficient use of resources, in line with project  objectives.  
193. Financial Reporting and Audits . Financial reports will be submitted to SCA, detailing the use of funds and 
ensuring compliance with GCF fiduciary standards. In addition to regular internal financial reviews, external audits 
will be conducted annually, with results shared with SCA and GCF to ensure transparency and accountability. 
These audits will ensure adherence to GCF’s financial and operational guidelines, ensuring that funds are being 
used as intended to achieve project goals.  
194. SCA will maintain oversight of financial controls across all countries and address any risks related to fund 
mismanagement through regular reviews, including spot checks, and by implementing corrective actions as 
needed.  
195. Procurement Arrangements . Procurement for the BRACE project will be guided by the procurement policies and 
procedures outlined in Annex 10: Procurement Plan . The procurement plan provides a high- level overview of the 
procurement methods and processes that will be used throughout the project, ensuring transparency and 
competition.  
196. Procurement capacity assessments confirmed  that SCI entities in Cambodia and South Sudan have procurement 
policies that align with SCA and meet GCF requirements. These entities will handle local procurement activities, 
ensuring procurement processes are competitive and transparent. In Tonga , where SCTON is a newly registered 
entity, procurement policies will be reviewed and aligned with SCA’s guidelines during the project’s start -up to 
ensure compliance with GCF standards.  
197. These EE will be responsible for executing procurement activities under the oversight of the PSCs, with strategic 
guidance from SCA. Larger and more complex procurement activities, such as:  School retrofitting  and climate-
resilient infrastructure improvements,  WASH infrastructure upgrades , including borehole rehabilitation and 
water -efficient systems,  Procurement of solar dryers  for agricultural use to support sustainable livelihoods,  will 
receive close oversight from SCA to ensure adherence to international best practices.  
198. Procurement risks were carefully assessed during the project design phase, with particular attention to countries 
with more challenging environments, such as South Sudan. The assessments identified potential risks related to 
political instability, logistic al challenges, and the potential for procurement delays. To mitigate these risks, a phased 
approach to procurement will be implemented, with high -risk activities subject to additional scrutiny and prior review 
by SCA. This will ensure that all procurement activities align with rigorous competitive bidding processes and SCA 
standards.

Secretariat’s assessment  of FP 274 
Proposal name:  Building the Climate Resilience of Children and Communities 
through the Education Sector (BRACE) 
Accredited entity:  Save the Children Australia  
Countries:  Cambodia, South Sudan, Tonga  
Project size:  Small  
I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat  
1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following 
remarks:  
Strengths  Points of caution  
Building the Climate Resilience of Children 
and Communities through the Education 
Sector ( BRACE) is the first GCF education-
focused project with an integrated and 
systemic way to enhance climate resilience. 
By integrating climate resilience measures into education systems, the project ensures educational continuity in the face of climate shocks, establishing a transformative approach to tackling climate change within the education sector.  The sustainability of BRACE’s interventions 
hinges on each country’s governance stability and financial ability to scale recommended practices.  
With a d irect beneficiary base of 608,193 
individuals ( 300,477 women) and an 
estimated 8.64 million indirect beneficiaries  
(4.2 million women) across Cambodia, South 
Sudan, and Tonga, BRACE demonstrates an expansive reach. The project prioritizes 
addressing critical challenges in education, 
including reducing absenteeism, preventing school closures, and mitigating dropout rates due to climate shocks. The combination of retrofitting school infrastructures, upgrading water, sanitation and hygiene ( WASH) 
systems, and introducing early warning mechanisms ensures resilience- building at 
multiple levels, directly benefiting some of the world’s most climate -vulnerable 
populations.  Political  instability and low government 
budget allocations could pose significant 
challenges  in South Sudan . Dependence on 
the readiness and availability of the 
respective m inistries of education to own the 
interventions post -project also introduces 
unpredictability.  
The initiative establishes knowledge -sharing 
platforms and learning spaces that enable 
cross- country collaboration and long- term Logistical challenges may exist  
in remote or conflict -affected areas,  
especially in South Sudan and Tonga.


---
*Page 123*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/ 02/Add. 16 
Page 123  
 
 
scalability.  The project’s design ensures that 
the generated insights and materials can be 
adapted and applied beyond the target countries, enhancing its global relevance.  
2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal  in accordance with the 
terms listed in the term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE), 
and, if  considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in annex II to document 
GCF/B. 42/02. 
II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment  
2.1 Project background  
3. Climate change poses an existential threat to education systems globally, with one 
billion children living in countries at extreme climate risk. The BRACE project brings innovative 
solutions by introducing the first GCF education- focused project and first comprehensive 
integration of climate resilience into education systems, combining climate -smart infrastructure 
improvements (e.g. infrastructure, WASH impacts, etc. ), educational continuity management 
systems, and curriculum reform. The project's unique ap proach addresses immediate climate 
impacts while also building long -term adaptive capacity through policy reform and institutional 
strengthening.  
4. The BRACE project will target two least developed countries (LDCs), South Sudan and 
Cambodia, and one small island developing State (SIDS), Tonga, that are exposed to increasing 
temperatures, changing rainfall patterns, and extreme events such as droughts, floods and high/extreme temperature events, which result in a range of education- related impacts.  
5. The project will achieve climate -resilient education systems through a multi -pronged 
approach:  
(a) Implement at -scale pilots in target countries:  implement climate -resilient education 
policies, retrofitted schools, safety systems and climate -focused curricula ; 
(b) Increased information and finance access:  assist education ministries in accessing 
climate finance, planning adaptation and creating finance road  maps ; and  
(c) Enhance coordination and learning : build a climate education financing platform, share 
best practices and promote South –South collaboration.  
6. This multi level  approach, combined with the project ’s USD 46.7 million investment and 
partnership with Global Partnership for Education (GPE), creates a replicable model for 
addressing climate impacts on education systems in highly vulnerable countries. The expected 
outcomes include increased resilience of 608,193 children and teachers in three countries 
directly and support for the respective ministries of e ducation, linking to the global education 
community and climate change actors to access climate fina nce and to lead climate -resilient 
sustainable development in the education sector, thus benefit ing 8,640,844  million people 
indirectly.  
7. The environmental and social safeguards (ESS) category for this project is C.  
2.2 Component -by-component analysis  
Component 1 : Building climate -resilient school infrastructure and systems  (total cost: USD 38.6 
million; GCF  cost:  USD 36 million)


---
*Page 124*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/ 02/Add. 16

8. Component 1 of the project is designed to enhance the resilience of the education sector 
in Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga in the face of current and future climate risks. It adopts a 
comprehensive, multi level  approach —engaging national, subnational, and school -level actors —
to establish the conditions necessary for long -term, scalable and sustainable transformation . 
(a) Output 1.1 : Enabling systems and policies are in place and supported  
(i) Activity 1.1.1 : Facilitate preparation and endorsement of climate and education 
policies, plans and guidelines ; 
(ii) Activity 1.1.2 : Strengthen coordination on climate change within ministries of 
education and other relevant line ministries at the national and subnational 
level ; 
(iii) Activity 1.1.3 : Facilitate integration of disaster risk reduction (DRR) into existing 
and/or new plans with education authorities ; and  
(iv) Activity 1.1.4 : Support children to participate in climate change and education 
policy initiatives . 
(b) Output 1.2 : School facilities are safer and greener  
(i) Activity 1.2.1 : Develop a national standard for climate -resilient school facilities;  
(ii) Activity 1.2.2 : Retrofit school facilities, including WASH and internet 
connectivity, to strengthen climate resilience ; and  
(iii) Activity 1.2.3 : Strengthen school communities and education officials to operate 
and maintain school infrastructure and facilities.  
(c) Output 1.3 School safety and educational continuity management systems are operating 
effectively  
(i) Activity 1.3.1 : Build capacity of national and subnational education officials to be 
master trainers on climate -related school safety ; 
(ii) Activity 1.3.2 : Build knowledge and capacity of school management, teachers 
and children to prepare climate -related school safety plans;  
(iii) Activity 1.3.3 : Implement school -level climate -related school safety plans ; 
(iv) Activity 1.3.4 : Provide climate resilience tools, equipment and kits to schools ; 
and 
(v) Activity 1.3.5 : Strengthen access to and use of early warning systems and climate 
information systems in schools.  
(d) Output 1.4: Climate change resilience teaching and learning embedded in national 
systems  
(i) Activity 1.4.1 : Develop teaching and learning materials on climate change ready 
to implement through national, non- formal, and informal curricula ; 
(ii) Activity 1.4.2 : Build knowledge and capacity of teachers and education managers 
to implement climate change lessons inside the classroom;  
(iii) Activity 1.4.3 : Strengthen the capacity of children’s  clubs to lead climate change 
initiatives in their school and community;  
(iv) Activity 1.4.4 : Disseminate climate change teaching and learning materials 
through e -learning and other platforms  of the ministries of education ; and

(v) Activity 1.4.5 : Prepare learning materials  and hold events with ministries of

into the curriculum . 
Component 2 : Enhancing access to climate finance for education  (total cost: USD 1.1 million; GCF  
cost:  USD 0.7 million)  
9. In component 2, the project will support the m inistries of education in the three

include the development of education sector risk analyses on the impact of climate change on 
the education sector and children’s learning, and the ministries of education engaging  in

the three countries, with the impact potential to reach children and education personnel in each 
country.

may feed into global decisions such as those of the UNFCCC.

projects.

III. Assessment of performance against  investment criteria  
3.1 Impact potential               Scale: N/A  
13. Under the component 1,  “Building climate -resilient school infrastructure and systems”,  
The total direct beneficiaries through this project will be 60 8,193 (comprising 299,933 females ) 
and total indirect beneficiaries will be 8,640,84 4 (comprising 4, 266, 850 females). It will also 
address climate impacts and risks in 850 schools. In Cambodia , the project targets at least 240 
primary schools across the provinces of Kampong Chhang, Kampong Cham, and Koh Kong, 
which are particularly vulnerable to extreme weather events.  In Cambodia it will directly reach 
392, 185 benefi ciaries (1 88,373 female), representing 2 .3 per cent of the population, and 
indirectly benefit 4,031,400 people (1,962,128 female), amounting to 24  per cent of the 
population. In South Sudan, the project targets children in 510 schools, directly benefiting an 
estimated 201, 242 people, including school children  and community members, with over half 
(52 per cent) being women and girls. Additionally, the project will impact estimated  4,572,682 
indirect beneficiaries  in South Sudan  (50 per cent female),. Direct beneficiaries account for 1.8  
per cent of the total population, while indirect beneficiaries make up 4 1 per cent. In Tonga , the 
project will enhance the climate resilience of 100 remote schools to address climate hazards. It will directly benefit  13,671 individuals (6 ,631 female), representing 13 per cent of the total


---
*Page 127*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/ 02/Add. 16 
Page 127  
 
 
population, and indirectly benefit 36,762 people (1 8,381 female), equivalent to 33 per cent of 
the population.  
14. Under component 2,  “Enhancing access to climate finance for education ”, the BRACE 
project aims to support the ministries of e ducation in the three target  countries by helping them 
access climate finance and lead climate -resilient sustainable development in the education 
sector. It will directly benefit210 ministry of education and government officials across these 
countries, with the potential to impact children and education personnel. Additionally, the CSESI 
project will complement these efforts by sharing lessons  learned from the BRACE  project  
implementation with  35 other countries.  
15. Under component 3, “Coordination and knowledge -sharing on climate and education” , 
more than 1,000 education and climate actors  and organizations  in each of the counties  will be 
benefited.  
3.2 Paradigm shift potential               Scale: N/A  
16. The BRACE project aims to create a paradigm shift in school safety and climate 
education by leveraging the Comprehensive School Safety Framework (CSSF). It focuses on improving school infrastructure, safety management systems, and enhancing climate change knowledge and skills among students. The project addresses barriers in governance, gender and 
social inclusion, and financial, technological and institutional areas. The project contributes to 
climate -resilient development pathways aligned with sustainable  development and educational 
strategies. It aims to increase climate knowledge and skills among children and teachers, enhance school safety and resilience, and strengthen disaster preparedness. Additionally, the 
BRACE project fosters collaboration between ministries of education and climate Change to 
access climate finance and embed climate -related policies.  
17. The BRACE project is designed for scalability and replicability, aligning with national 
policy objectives and complementing other climate -focused projects. It aims to expand the CSSF 
model to more schools, enhancing infrastructure, safety management systems and climate 
education. The project will provide materials such as standards, guidelines and plans to facilitate adaptation in different contexts. Additionally, climate finance tools and resources developed through the project will further support its sca lability and replicability across various 
regions.  
18. The BRACE project emphasizes knowledge -sharing and learning through dedicated 
components focused on school safety. It involves annual workshops, a learning agenda shared 
across three countries, and e -learning platforms for broader dissemination . Components 2 and 
3 focus on scaling up climate action in education by applying the CSSF model in various 
contexts. Component 2 and 3  promote cross -country learning and capacity -building for 
ministries of education on climate finance, engaging in UNFCCC processes lik e NAP 
development. Knowledge products will be disseminated through dedicated platforms, webinars 
and learning events.  The BRACE project is positioned to test new interventions on a moderate 
scale and share findings widely.  
3.3 Sustainable development potential              Scale: N/A  
19. The BRACE project aims to deliver significant gender and social inclusion benefits, 
indirectly impacting over 4.1 million females. It focuses on fostering gender equality and social 
inclusion (GESI) within the education sector through interventions that pr omote social attitude 
and behavioural  changes. The project engages stakeholders and facilitates learning 
opportunities to advocate for gendered climate needs and actions. Key initiatives include providing gendered, age -appropriate, and disability -friendly W ASH facilities, which can also


---
*Page 128*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/ 02/Add. 16 
Page 128  
 
 
serve the community during extreme weather The project promotes menstrual hygiene and 
provides dignity kits for girls and teachers, enhancing community capacity. Improved W ASH 
facilities near schools and communities aim to reduce the burden on women and girls related to 
water collection and washing. The BRACE project empowers communities by forming DRR 
committees involving children, women and people with disabilities in remote areas. It incorporates GESI needs into risk assessments, DRR plans and climate -related school safety 
plans. Early warning systems will use language that is responsive to age, gender and disabilities.  
20. The BRACE project also aims to achieve environmental co- benefits by implementing 
school greening initiatives. These include reducing emissions through improved infrastructure and using photovoltaic (PV) panels for lighting and connectivity,  and natural resource 
management efforts, such as tree planting and water -efficient irrigation systems. Supporting 
school food gardens can encourage the community to adopt positive environmental practices. Additionally, the project enhances environmental impact through i mproved W ASH facilities, 
waste management systems and hygiene practices . 
21. The BRACE project enhances social co -benefits by promoting locally  led adaptation 
(LLA) at the school level. It engages children, teachers and community members to influence the LLA agenda through school safety plans, child ren’s  clubs, and participation in the development 
and implementation of NAPs. School -led initiatives and knowledge -sharing amplify LLA efforts, 
offering advocacy opportunities at climate events like United Nations Climate Change 
Conferences . This ground -up approach empowers children as ke y stakeholders in climate action 
and decision -making, aligning with LLA principles.  
22. The project also contributes to the Sustainable Development Goals, particularly goals 4 
(Quality Education), 5 (Gender Equality), 6 ( Clean Water and Sanitation), 11 ( Sustainable Cities 
and Communities ) and 13 (Climate Action).  
3.4 Needs of the recipient               Scale: N/A  
23. The climate change vulnerabilities affect school infrastructure, learning  and health, and 
have broader economic and social impacts, highlighting the need for climate -resilient 
investments. In Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga, climate hazards such as droughts, heatwaves, wildfires, rising sea levels and storms increase vulnerability,  exacerbated by 
insufficient financial investment in education systems. To build a climate -informed society, the 
education sector must integrate climate resilience into its planni ng and operations. This 
includes constructing resilient infrastructure, developing DRR  strategies, incorporating climate 
change into curricula, enhancing teachers’  knowledge and capacity, and supporting community 
adaptation efforts.  
24. Cambodia, one of the poorest countries  in South -East Asia , is particularly exposed to 
floods, droughts, heatwaves, cyclones and seawater intrusion, which damage school 
infrastructure, disrupt education and pose health risks to school communities. Cambodia ranks 
46th in UNICEF’s Children’s Climate Risk Index, indicating a high risk to children from water 
scarcity, flooding and vector -borne diseases. The Global Climate Risk Index places Cambodia as 
the 14th most climate risk -prone country globally, highlighti ng the urgent need for climate -
resilient strategies and investments.  
25. South Sudan, a n LDC, faces significant vulnerability to climate shocks and has urgent 
adaptation needs. It ranks as the 8th most vulnerable country globally according to the Notre 
Dame Global Adaptation Index , and 175th in readiness to adapt to climate change impacts. South 
Sudan ’s lowland and swampy topography make schools and communities highly susceptible to 
worsening flooding due to erratic rainfall, leading to school closures and severe infrastructure 
damage.


---
*Page 129*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/ 02/Add. 16 
Page 129  
 
 
26. Tonga ’s education system faces significant challenges from climate change, including sea 
level rise, droughts, floods, tropical cyclones and heatwaves, with schools, particularly on outer 
islands, needing urgent infrastructural support. The lack of climate -speci fic courses in schools 
highlights the need to prepare students for climate -related professions. Past climate events have 
disrupted education and incurred high financial costs, with a missing climate focus in previous projects. Funding sources are insufficient to meet the adaptation needs of the dispersed school system, with international programmes  often focusing on immediate development rather than 
climate change.  
3.5 Country ownership                Scale: N/A  
27. The BRACE project aligns with the national climate change strategies and educational 
policies of Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga to support the development of climate -resilient 
education systems. It aligns with South Sudan ’s NAP and nationally determined contributions  
(NDCs), the Cambodia Climate Change Strategic Plan and NAP -DRR, and Tonga ’s Strategic 
Development Framework and Joint National Action Plan 2 on  Climate Change Adaptation and 
Disaster Risk Management , emphasizing climate adaptation and resilien ce in education.  This 
project is also designed to be gender -responsive and inclusive, addressing the needs of girls, 
children with disabilities, women and internally displaced people. It aligns with South Sudan’ s 
National Gender Policy, Cambodia ’s Gender Mainstreaming Strategic Plan in Climate Change and 
Disaster Risk Management, and Tonga ’s national education policy framework, integrating 
gender perspectives into climate adaptation and DRR . By ensuring equal access to climate -
resilient educational faciliti es, the BRACE project supports inclusive interventions across all 
genders and children with disabilities in these countries.  
28. In each country, BRACE engages local governments, community organizations and 
school management committees , ensuring that interventions are contextually relevant and 
sustainable. By fostering a sense of ownership among local stakeholders the project ensures that the benefits of climate resilience are maintained long after the project’s completion. Stakeholder e ngagement has been crucial in the development and implementation of the BRACE 
project across Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga. Extensive consultations  with government 
agencies, local communities, non- governmental organizations  (NGOs) and development 
partners have ensured the project aligns with local needs, focusing on school infrastructure 
resilience and climate education in South Sudan, identifying key intervention areas in Cambodia, and addressing local climate challenges in Tonga. These participatory processes have shaped the project ’s design, fostered broad support, and will continue to involve stakeholders 
throughout implementation, ensuring alignment with national and community priorities and 
allowing for adaptive management based on feedback.  
3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness      Scale:  N/A  
29. The BRACE project is expected to directly benefit 608,193individuals and indirectly 
benefit over 8.6 million people through improved education infrastructure and system- wide 
resilience measures . It targets the most climate -vulnerable populations in the education sector, 
aiming to reduce disruptions to schooling due to extreme weather, enhance the safety and utility of school facilities and embed climate ri sk awareness and adaptation capacity into the 
education system. The concessionality of a GCF grant is p roposed due to the countries’ limited 
fiscal space, the public- good nature of basic education, and the lack of private sector incentives 
in this space. The grant allows the pilot countries to implement transformational measure s that 
would otherwise remain unfunded. The project’s results are designed to be replicable, 
contributing to long -term learning and scalability in other climate -vulnerable education 
systems.


---
*Page 130*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/ 02/Add. 16 
Page 130  
 
 
30. The project’s cost –benefit analysis shows that investing in resilient school facilities and 
education system strengthening  generates high social and economic returns. The project 
estimates an economic internal rate of return (EIRR) of 35– 36 per cent  and an economic net 
present value of appr oximately USD 87 million over a 15-year period based on a 9  per cent  
discount rate. These figures are consistent across both Representative Concentration Pathway 
(RCP) 4.5 and RCP  8.5 climate scenarios, indicating strong economic viability and resilience to 
climate uncertainty. The EIRR reflects a combination of increased economic returns from 
improved human capital, reduced costs associated with school infrastructure damage from 
climate -related events, fewer missed sch ooldays  and additional benefits from school safety and 
curriculum tr aining. While some benefit assumption s are based on field interview s and expert 
inputs, sensitivity anal ysis was applied to test the robustness of results. Even under 
conservative scenarios, the project maintains an EIRR well above the generally accepted 10  per 
cent  threshold.  
31. The BRACE project proposes concessional financing due to the critical need for such 
funding in the three pilot countries, which are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. This financing is essential for implementing interventions that improve the educ ational environment 
and foster positive societal change. Basic education, considered a public good, is typically funded by governments, as its attributes make it challenging for private firms to profit, meaning 
private sector displacement is not a concern. The investment in education is crucial for societal benefit, as it leads to a more educated population and enhances public welfare.  
IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  
4.1 Environmental and social safeguards  
32. The project aims to increase the climate resilience of the education sector and its 
stakeholders in the target countries, both at the national and subnational  levels by capacitating 
existing institutions, enhancing access to finance and climate information, and providing cross -
border learning opportunities. The main physical activities of the project that may have minor 
occupational health and safety risks and/or impacts are related to retrofitting of school facilities 
including improving the cooling systems and ventilation, installing solar PV systems to improve 
access to renewable energy for lighting and internet connectivity, improving shade through tree 
planting within the school area, installing rainwater harvesting structures and upgrading 
drinki ng water systems. The AE has developed an Environmental and Social Assessment and 
Residual Risk Management Plan (ESARMP)  to mitigate the risks of the project. The management 
measures include ensuring workers will be provided with appropriate training on how to use 
tools, equipment and materials prior to commencement of works  and will be provided with 
appropriate personal protective equipment . A PMU , which will have a combined ESS, GESI and 
Engagement Specialist to implement the ESARMP , will also be established.   
33. The AE has conducted consultations in the target countries involving government 
ministries, NGOs, civil society organizations , multilateral organizations , donors and the private 
sector in the development of the project to ensure it aligns with the needs and priorities of each 
country’s education sector as far as climate resilience and disaster preparedness are concerned. A stakeholder engagement plan is developed,  and a grievance redress mechanism will also be 
put in place.  
34. Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) . The revised GCF Environmental 
and Social Policy adopted by decision B.BM- 2021/18 requires safeguarding from SEAH in GCF-
financed activities. The AE provided SEAH safeguarding in its submission to this funding 
proposal. As a standard practice, the AE conducts SEAH risk assessments for all its projects 
including country -specific assessment for this project. Key SEAH safeguarding measures have


---
*Page 131*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/ 02/Add. 16 
Page 131  
 
 
been included in the SEAH grievance mechanism in alignment with the approach of the AE to 
child protection and a SEAH/ gender -based violence continuum of care, while employing a 
survivor -cent red approach. The rights, needs and wishes of survivors (or victims) will be the 
foremost priority in this  project. The  survivors’ rights to dignity, privacy and confidentiality, 
safety, non- discrimination and access to information key aspects of the SEAH protection  and 
response , including violence against children, and gender -based violence cases.  
35. GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy & ESS7 (Indigenous Peoples): The target countries 
and project sites have populations considered Indigenous ( Cambodia, South Sudan and  Tonga ).  
The project acknowledges that while there are Indigenous Peoples or minority ethnic  groups  at 
the project site intervention level, school communities are homogeneous in language, culture 
and practices. Thus, project benefits or impacts will not adversely affect Indigenous People s 
under the GCF definition at the project site level. All physical activities will take place within 
existing defined school footprints where the school has documented a lease or secure usage 
rights of that land. Therefore, the project does not require free, prior, and informed consent. No 
lands or resources used by Indig enous People s or other minority groups will be subject to any 
policies or plans developed by the project. The project has integrated traditional customary 
processes for each project country into the p roject design and stakeholder engagement. The AE 
has given assurances that the material developed will be culturally appropriate and sensitive. 
This approach ensures the careful involvement and consideration of Indigenous Peoples 
without adversely impacting their lifestyles or resources. The requirements of the  GCF 
Indigenous Peoples Policy are included in the environmental and social action plan  and so no 
Indigenous Peoples plan or Indigenous Peoples planning framework was required. In line with 
its roles and functions, the Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to 
the AE and executing entities  (EEs) . In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF 
Indigenous Peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.  
4.2 Gender policy  
36. The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and 
therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Updated Gender Policy. The gender assessment and action plan were prepared by the project feasibility teams and GESI specialists in the target  countries of Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga. The AE also ensure d that this 
process aligned with their gender mainstreaming guidelines, their gender equality and inclusion 
policies, and country level Save the Children International  gender equality and inclusion action 
plans. A detailed analysis was made of all five key assessment domains, namely: laws, policies 
and institutions; social norms, time use; participation and leadership; access to and control over 
resources and services; and safety, dignity and well -being.  The gaps identified in each of the 
domains have corresponding recommendations in alignment with the BRACE project.  
37. The AE submitted a comprehensive gender action plan (GAP) to mainstream gender and 
social inclusion throughout the BRACE project. Under component 1, “Building climate -resilient 
school infrastructure and systems” , among other GESI activities, the project will facilitate the 
review and adoption of school design that is green, safe, healthy, inclusive and gender -
responsive, in alignment with the national guidelines which provide for inclusive approaches; 
and inclusion of GESI considerations in climate -related school safety training and the 
differential impacts of climate change on accessing education for different groups. In addition to 
this, the infrastructure and WASH assessments will include age, gender and disability -specific 
needs and identify any differentiated risks. Under component 2 , Enhancing access to climate 
finance for education, the project will support various capacity -building initiatives such as 
gender -related cross- sectoral coordination with key line ministries; and education- focused GESI 
conte nt in NAPs and NDCs. Regarding  component 3, on “Coordination and knowledge -sharing 
on climate and education ”, the project will, among other  gender strategic activitie s, map, track


---
*Page 132*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/ 02/Add. 16 
Page 132  
 
 
and promote GESI opportunities within climate finance for education; and the project  will 
include evidence and learning on GESI in all the knowledge management platforms for the 
project. Each GESI activity has detailed gender indicators for each component in the GAP which will help in monitoring and evaluation of the gender and social outcomes for this project.  
4.3 Risks  
4.3.1.  Overall project/ programme assessment ( medium  risk)  
38. The project aims to establish climate -resilient education  systems in two LDCs, South 
Sudan and Cambodia, and one SIDS, Tonga, that are exposed to increasing  average  
temperatures, changing rainfall patterns, and extreme events such as droughts, floods and high/extreme temperature events. The GCF is requested to provide a full grant of USD 46.7 
million. GPE, UNICEF and World Bank are contributing co- financing of USD 6 million in the form 
of grants.  
4.3.2.  Accredited entity /executing entity capability to execute the current 
project/ programme ( medium  risk)  
39. The AE has an extensive track record and global experience in implementing climate 
adaptation projects. This funding proposal represents the first GCF- funded project to focus on 
mitigating climate risk in the education sector.  
4.3.3.  Project/programme -specific execution risks (medium  risk)  
40. Political instability:  political instability  arising from conflicts, governance challenges, 
and changes in political leadership  in South Sudan and Cambodia could deprioritize climate 
resilience initiatives. Mitigation measures can involve  mak ing the project more locally led or 
better integrated within the local government system, so that even during instances of  national -
level unrest the project can still operate at the community  level, as the PMU is often located 
within the community or project sites.  
4.3.4.  Compliance risk ( medium  risk)   
41. The project presents a moderate compliance risk. Procurement and financial 
management gaps in newly established entities, such as Save the Children Tonga (SCTON), expose the project to risks of misallocation and non- compliance due to underdeveloped systems 
and insufficient inter nal controls. To mitigate this, tailored capacity -building efforts are planned 
to align their processes with GCF- specific requirements, coupled with ongoing monitoring to 
ensure adherence to financial management standards and regulatory compliance. While no immediate reasons for concern are apparent, thorough due diligence remains essential to mitigate any latent risks that could arise from evolving legal frameworks or geopolitical factors.  
42. A multi -layered compliance framework is in place to mitigate these risks, leveraging 
anti -money- laundering and countering the financing of terrorism (AML/CFT) policies, enhanced 
due diligence on all partners, real -time sanctions screening, and financial oversight 
mechanisms. Digital financial tracking tools will be used to enhance transparency, while 
mandatory fraud prevention and ethics training will strengthen internal controls. Regular 
audits, spot checks, and ongoing partner assessments will ensure comp liance with procurement 
and financial integrity standards. A whistle -blower mechanism is also in place to encourage 
reporting of suspicious activities, ensuring accountability across all levels of project execution.  
43. Residual risk remains at a manageable level, contingent on continuous oversight and 
adaptive compliance strategies. Based on the implemented mitigation measures, the residual


---
*Page 133*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/ 02/Add. 16 
Page 133  
 
 
compliance risk is assessed as Medium Risk. Continuous monitoring, adaptive risk management, 
and stringent enforcement of financial compliance policies will be critical in maintaining project integrity and alignment with GCF fiduciary standards.  
4.3.5.  GCF portfolio concentration risk   
44. In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF concentration risk remains 
within the monitoring thresholds of the Risk Appetite Statement in terms of results areas, single proposal or AE concentration.

4.4 Fiduciary  
45. SCA is the AE for this project. The EEs are SCA, Save the Children International in 
Cambodia (SCIKHM), SCTON, and  the, Save the Children International in South Sudan (SCISSD) , 
Ministry of Education, Youth and Sport ( MoEYS).  
46. The AML/CFT, financial systems, procedures, and country capacity assessments for the 
BRACE project had been done by the AE on all EEs, detailed in annex 20 to the funding proposal . 
In all three countries, funds will be distributed to EEs and implementing partners, based on work plans  and budgets approved by the project steering committees.  
47. SCA will not manage the co- financing from UNICEF or the World Bank. A joint oversight 
mechanism in South Sudan will manage the GPE funding. Financial reports will be submitted to SCA, detailing the use of funds and ensuring compliance with GCF fiduciary standards. External audits will be conducted annually, with results shared with SCA and GCF to ensure transparency and accountability.  
48. SCA will maintain oversight of financial controls across all countries and address any 
risks related to fund mismanagement through regular reviews, including spot checks, and by implementing corrective actions as needed.  
49. The GCF account for the BRACE project will be managed by SCA as the AE. SCA will 
transfer GCF funds to project -specific bank accounts in Cambodia, South Sudan and Tonga. 
These accounts will be managed by the respective project implementation units, which will 
oversee the use of funds for project implementation, including disbursements to government ministries, implementing partners such as the United Nations Educational, Scientific and 
Cultural Organization , and other procured entities.  
4.5 Results monitoring and reporting 
50. This is an adaptation project that will contribute to 3 of the 4 GCF adaptation results 
areas , such as ARA1 (Most vulnerable people and communities), ARA2 ( Health, well- being, food 
and water security), and ARA3 ( Infrastructure and built environment). The project is expected 
to reach 608,193 direct beneficiaries  and more than 8.6 million indirect beneficiaries  in 
Cambodia and Tonga  in the Asia and the Pacific region, and in South Sudan in the Africa region.


---
*Page 134*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/ 02/Add. 16 
Page 134  
 
 
51. The results management arrangements proposed under the project were refined over 
five rounds of review and engagement with the AE. Specifically, changes were made to the 
project ’s theory of change to ensure that it was clear, logically coherent, and linked to the 
climate rationale and activities proposed under the project.  
52. The project ’s logical framework was also refined over multiple rounds to ensure that the 
proposed indicators and targets were realistic and linked to the proposed theory of change. In 
particular, the AE was given feedback to ensure that ARA -level beneficiary targets were 
accurately articulated against identified adaptation benefits , and  that  there was low risk of 
inaccurate or double -counting of beneficiaries. It is positive to note that the AE has defined 
project -level outcome and output indicators, in add ition to the indicators against various GCF 
result areas.  
53. The AE was also provided with advice for refining the supporting annexes  to the funding 
proposal , including the Implementation Timetable, Monitoring and Evaluation Plan, and 
beneficiary calculations. Guidance was provided to the AE to ensure that the beneficiary calculations, especially for indirect beneficiaries,  are realistic and aligned with the proposed 
project rationale and activities.  
54. The AE responded positively to the guidance provided and refined the funding proposal 
accordingly.  
4.6 Legal assessment  
55. The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 20 
December 2019 (the “AMA” ), and it became effective on 20 May 2020. The Accredited Entity’s 
five- year accreditation term was lapsed on 19 May 2025 but was extended by Decision B.37/18, 
paragraph (q) until the earlier of three years from the date of lapse of the five -year accreditati on 
term or the date on which a revised accreditation framework is adopted by the Board.  
56. The Accredited Entity has provided a legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all 
internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.  
57. The proposed project will be implemented in the Kingdom of Cambodia, the Republic of 
South Sudan and the Kingdom of Tonga. The GCF has signed bilateral agreements on privileges and immunities with the Kingdom of Tonga. In the case of the Kingdom of Cambodi a and the 
Republic of South Sudan, GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, 
amongst other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in these countries, 
which risks need to be further assessed. Moreover, th e ability of GCF to undertake redress 
activities and/or investigations in such countries may be hindered due to the absence of privileges and immunities for relevant GCF personnel.  
58. Therefore, it is recommended that the Board considers whether disbursements of GCF 
proceeds should only be made after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation 
and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privil eges and 
immunities for GCF and its personnel.  
59. To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by 
the Board is made subject to the following conditions:  
(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and  
(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP274 
Proposal name:  Building the Climate Resilience of Children and Communities 
through the Education Sector (BRACE)  
Accredited entity : Save the Children Australia 
Countries : Cambodia, S outh Sudan, Tonga  
Project size:  Small 
I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
1.1 Overview  
1. The submission of this funding proposal for the multi -country “Building the Climate 
Resilience of Children and Communities through the Education Sector  (BRACE )” project is 
under the G reen Climate Fun d (GCF) standard proposal approval process, for a small -size 
public -sector adaptation programme in environmental and social safeguards risk C ategory C.1  
2. BRACE will focus on strengthening  climate resilience in the education sector in three 
countries namely: two least developed countries  of South Sudan and Cambodia, and the  small 
island developing State of Tonga.  
3. The BRACE initiativ e was co nceived during a climate finance dialogue in the margins of 
the twenty -eighth Conference of the Parties (COP  28) of the United Nations Framework 
Convention on Climate Change  (annex 2, appendix 4) .2 The dialogue was convened by Save the 
Children, the GCF and the Global Partnership for Education (GPE)3 and was aimed at 
accelerating climate action by linking the education and climate agendas. Consequently GPE is 
providing USD 21 million to support 35 countries to integrate climate change into the education 
sector through the Climate Smart Education Systems Initiative ( CSESI) .4 
4. The three countries participating in the BRACE project are exposed to increasing 
temperatures, changing rainfall patterns, sea level rise,5 and extreme events such as droughts, 
floods, and high/extreme temperature events, which result in a range of impacts  on the 
education sector.  School buildings, water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH) facilities, and school 
materials are damaged or destroyed during extreme weather events or slowly eroded by 
harsher climate conditions.  
 
1 The assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) is based on the funding proposal received on 
16 April 2025  (updated with minor modifications on 12 May 2025 ) and is informed by a set of written questions 
and answers further discussed in an online meeting between the iTAP and the accredited entity on 8 May 2025.  
2 COP  28 refers to the United Nations Climate Change Conference that took place in Dubai, United Arab Emirates, from 
30 November to 12 December 2023.  
3 The Global Partnership for Education (GPE), a multi -stakeholder partnership hosted by the World Bank, supports 
governments in low -income countries to transform their education systems through technical support, financial 
resources, and facilitating collab oration among stakeholders. 
4 The United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization and Save the Children Australia are the 
implementing partners of the GPE  CSESI.  
https://www.globalpartnership.org/node/document/download?file=document/file/2024 -06-climate -smart-
education -systems -initiative -bi-annual -progress -report.pdf   
5 As a landlocked country, South Sudan is not directly affected by sea  level rise.

*Page 136*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add.1 6 
Page 136 
 
 
5. School disruptions (from climate and non -climate drivers) can affect children’s 
attendance and learning outcomes, increase the likelihood of children dropping out of schools, 
and affect physical safety and psychosocial well- being.  Girls  are likely to face higher risks of 
permanently dropping out of school , increases in gender -based violence , and early marriage.   
6. The funding proposal identifies common barriers to climate change adaptation in the 
education sector in all three countries to include limited institutional capacities and weak inter -
agency coordination, limited access to climate information for decision -making, low levels of 
technical expertise to support timely action , and financial constraints.   
7. The funding proposal aims to integrate climate resilience into education systems, by 
adopting an approach that addresses immediate climate impacts while building long -term 
adaptive capacity through policy reform and institutional strengthening.  The pro ject will be 
delivered through three inter -linked components as follows:  
(a) Component 1: Building climate -resilient school infrastructure and s ystems  will 
support enabling climate -resilient education policies, support 850 schools to become 
safer and greener, strengthen school safety and management, and embed climate -
focused curricula in national education systems.  
(b) Component 2: Enhancing access to c limate finance for e ducation  will assist the 
education sector to access finance for climate action, creat e finance road  map s, and 
support  climate risk -informed decision -making . 
(c) Component 3: Coordination and knowledge -sharing on climate and education  will 
build climate -education co -investment platform s and  facilitate peer learning and South -
South collaboration between the three participating countries.   
8.  The budget of USD 46,737,340  comprises a request for GCF grant funds of USD 
40,751,264  with co -financing of USD 5,986,076 , with an implementation period of 5 years and 
an overall benefit stream of 10 years. Parallel finance through GPE CSESI  will complement 
efforts undertaken by BRACE, specifically for Components 2 and 3, thereby helping to sustain, scale and replicate BRACE approaches for other countries.  
9. The accredited entity (AE) is Save the Children Australia (SCA) , an international non -
governmental organi zation registered in Australia.  SCA will act as the executing entity (EE) for 
Components 2 and 3. The EEs for component 1 in each country include:  Save the Children 
International , registered in Cambodia  as an  international non- governmental organization ; and 
Royal Government of Cambodia, acting through the Ministry of Education, Youth and Sport  
(Cambodia): Save the Children International , registered in South Sudan  as an internation al non-
governmental organization  (South Sudan) ; and  Save the Children Tonga Trust , registered  in 
Tonga  as an  international non- governmental organi zation  (Tonga) . 
10. There are separate governance and management arrangements for each of the three 
countries , including national -level project steering committees with multi -stakeholder 
participation .  Additionally, project implementation units will be overseen by the respective 
Save the Children entities in each of the three countries.  
1.2 Impact potential               Scale : Medium  to High  
11. Overview:  BRACE proposes to tackle challenges of climate -related increases in children 
dropping out of school, absenteeism of children and teachers from school, learning loss and impacts on learning outcomes, gender -related impacts , and the physical impacts climate 
hazards have on school facilities.   
12. Country selection:  The AE clarified to the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
(iTAP ), that BRACE drew from CSESI’s selection of 35 countries that was based on climate risk


---
*Page 137*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add.1 6 
Page 137 
 
 
and vulnerability using three  climate risk indices and the Human Development I ndex.  BRACE 
further identified the three participating countries from across different geographies and 
contexts  based on government willingness , ability to participate,  and the status of the country’s 
GCF pipeline . By successfully demonstrating the approach of integrating climate change in the 
education sector across different contexts, the AE intends that the BRACE approach could be 
replicated across other CSESI countries.  
13. The iTAP sought clarification on how conflict and fragility informed country selection  
and project implementation . The AE explained that a Fragility Context Analysis for 2025 has 
been prepared for South Sudan, which includes an analysis of current political instability and 
conflict, humanitarian needs, and economic factors that contribute to the country’s fragility. The 
AE proposes to use the Conflict Sensitivity Guider6 established by SCA  and explained how 
BRACE will apply a conflict sensitivity approach throughout the project cycle.   
14. Selection of schools : Target areas in each participating country were selected based on 
climate and socioeconomic vulnerability, synergies with other interventions, and key stakeholder consultations.  This includes three provinces in Cambodia , six counties in six states 
in South Sudan , and schools across Tonga with a focus on those situated in the outer islands.   
15. The selection of 850 schools is based on eligibility criteria and the final list will be 
verified with the respective governments and with other development partners to ensure there 
is no overlap. The  iTAP also understands that these numbers may be subject to some 
adjustment as the school selection across the three countries will be confirmed during the 
inception phase.  The eligibility and prioritization criteria for school s selected, as covered in the 
feasibility study (annex 2), are comprehensive and include: climate risks vulnerability  and 
exposure to multiple climate- related hazards ; geographic location; p oor/ unsafe infrastructure 
condition s; large student p opulation , especially those from marginalized or vulnerable 
communities;  and high ed ucational needs . BRACE has also considered alignment with 
stakeholder priorities, ensuring that initiatives are gendered , culturally appropriate and 
sustainable , supporting equity and inclusivity by targeting schools serving disadvantaged or 
marginalized populations, including girls and ch ildren with disabilities, and by taking into 
consideration potential for collaboration and complementarity with other initiatives.  
16. Climate change exposure, risk  and  vulnerability:  The AE has confirmed  to the iTAP  
that climate risk assessment s, including geographic information system mapping, have been 
completed in each project area and are included in the country sections of the feasibility study 
(annex 2).  Further national and site -specific assessments linked to addressing present and 
projected climate -related vulnerabilities, impacts and risks in each country are planned at early 
stages of project implementation.  
17. The funding proposal clearly sets out the climate -related vulnerabilities in each of the 
three participating countries  under the Shared Socioeconomic Pathways scenarios of SSP2 –4.5 
from 2020 to 2050 .  Annex 2, a ppendix 1, succinctly frames the climate drivers (e.g. , 
high/extreme temperature events, rainfall variability, sea level rise) and the non-climate drivers  
(e.g. , geographic location, poverty, socioeconomic vulnerabilities, deforestation, gender and 
disability). The types of climate -related impacts affecting school community vulnerabilities (i.e. 
children, teachers, infrastructure) are outlined in a table (page 10 of the funding proposal).  
18. Cambodia, South Sudan, and Tonga are projected to face significant climate impacts, 
including rising temperatures, extreme weather, and changing rainfall patterns. In Cambodia 
and South Sudan , increased heat poses serious risks to school -age children  and heighten the 
risks of heat -related illnesses, dehydration, and reduced concentration . Prolonged exposure to 
extreme temperatures can also disrupt sleep, affecting children’s cognitive function and

which threaten children’s well- being . 
19. Adaptation results areas : Programme impacts are listed under  three GCF adaptation 
results areas, as follows:  
(a) Most vulnerable people and communities  (50 per cent of GCF contribution);  
(b) Health, food and water securit y (25 per cent of GCF contribution);  and 
(c) Infrastructure and built environment ( 25 per cent of GCF contribution) . 
20. The project anticipates reaching 607,098  direct beneficiaries and 8,640,844  indirect 
beneficiaries (see table 1 below for details) . This will mainly be delivered through C omponent 1 , 
includes 210 direct beneficiaries comprising government officials to be reached through 
Component 2, and 1,000 climate and education actors through Component 3.

21. Beneficiaries : The beneficiary calculations, presented in a nnexes 17a and 17b, are well 
detailed , draw on available country school census data,7 and explain the underlying 
assumptions for the calculations.  Direct beneficiaries include children, teachers, school 
management committees, education officials and parents and community members, while 
indirect beneficiaries are wider coverage of all other remaining school -aged children and 
communities that could broadly benefit from early warning systems and climate information 
systems.  The iTAP has observed a slight variance in the total numbers of direct beneficiaries as 
presented in the main funding proposal (which states 607,098 people) compared to the annexes 17a and 17b (which states 608,193 people).  
22. The funding proposal sets out how the 850 targeted schools will be supported through 
output areas under Component 1.  Overall, 77 schools will benefit from climate -resilient 
infrastructure improvements, including water and sanitation facilities (output 1.2), 400 schools will benefit from school safety and educational continuity management (output 1.3), and 800 
schools will be targ eted for climate change resilience teaching and learning (output 1.4).  
23. Tables in t he annexes highlight additional adaptation benefits across the components 
and outputs of the BRACE project, for direct and indirect beneficiaries across all three countries. 
 
7 Annex 17b clarifies that data may be subject to change according to updates  by the ministries of ed ucation in the
